The results are most informative for predicting the effect of punishment in large, anonymous, repeated PGGs with reputation tracking, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency.

- source: Skatova, A., & Ferguson, E. (2013). Individual differences in behavioural inhibition explain free riding in public good games when punishment is expected but not implemented. *Behavioral and Brain Functions*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both the implementation and the expectation of punishment (even when not actually implemented) increase individual contributions in a one-shot PGG compared to standard PGGs without punishment. Implemented punishment leads to higher contributions than non-implemented punishment. Individual differences in BIS-anxiety (a measure of behavioral inhibition) predict higher contributions and less free riding when punishment is expected but not implemented. FFFS (flight, fight, freeze system) does not predict contributions. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution levels, free riding), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that both implemented and expected (but not implemented) centralized punishment increase contributions in a one-shot PGG, with implemented punishment having a stronger effect. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes due to punishment. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in contribution rates under different punishment regimes and for understanding the role of individual differences in behavioral inhibition. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that enabling punishment (even if not always implemented) increases cooperation, but the magnitude of efficiency gains cannot be directly inferred from the reported outcomes.

- source: Villatoro, D., Andrighetto, G., Brandts, J., Nardin, L. G., Sabater-Mir, J., & Conte, R. (2014). The Norm-Signaling Effects of Group Punishment: Combining Agent-Based Simulation and Laboratory Experiments. *Social Science Computer Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when punishment is inflicted by the entire group, human subjects are more likely to cooperate (i.e., contribute to the public good) than when punishment is uncoordinated, even though the material cost to defectors is held constant. The authors attribute this to the norm-signaling function of group punishment, which is hypothesized to activate both instrumental and normative motivations for compliance. Agent-based simulations support the idea that norm salience, as modeled in the EMIL-I-A architecture, can explain the observed human behavior. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (contribution rates).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of punishment (specifically, whether it is coordinated by the group or uncoordinated) can significantly affect cooperation rates in public goods games, even when the material incentives are held constant. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper suggests that group punishment may lead to higher efficiency than uncoordinated punishment, but this is inferred from contribution rates rather than directly measured. The absence of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to calibrate quantitative predictions of efficiency gains from punishment. The findings are most useful for understanding the qualitative impact of group punishment structure and norm-signaling on cooperation, which may be relevant for predicting efficiency when combined with other sources.

- source: Bortolotti, S., Casari, M., & Pancotto, F. (2015). NORMS OF PUNISHMENT: EXPERIMENTS WITH STUDENTS AND THE GENERAL POPULATION. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The opportunity for peer punishment increases cooperation among students but not among the general population. In the student sample, average contributions rise substantially when punishment is available (from 6.8 to 12.8 out of 20), while in the representative sample, contributions are slightly lower with punishment (from 9.1 to 8.9). The general population punishes as much or more than students, but punishment is more often directed at cooperators (antisocial punishment), and recipients are less responsive to punishment. As a result, punishment fails to increase cooperation or efficiency in the general population. Earnings per period decrease with punishment in both groups, but more so in the representative sample. The study highlights that the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency is highly dependent on the subject pool, with punishment only improving cooperation (and likely efficiency) among students.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated PGGs depends critically on the subject pool. In a standard 4-player, 8-round PGG with MPCR=0.5 and a punishment technology of 1 token cost to reduce 4 tokens from another, punishment increases cooperation (and likely efficiency) among students but not among a representative general population. The main moderator is the prevalence of antisocial punishment and the responsiveness to punishment. For prediction, if the game is run with a student sample, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency; with a general population sample, punishment may not improve and could even reduce efficiency due to antisocial punishment and lack of response. The paper does not report efficiency directly, but the pattern of contributions and earnings suggests these conclusions.

- source: Nelissen, R. M. A., & Mulder, L. B. (2013). What makes a sanction stick? The effects of financial and social sanctions on norm compliance. *Social Influence*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both financial and social sanctions increase cooperation (measured as average contribution) in a repeated public goods game compared to a no-sanction control. Financial sanctions produce higher contributions than social sanctions while the sanctioning opportunity is present. However, after the removal of sanctions, contributions drop much more in the financial sanction condition than in the social sanction or control conditions. Social sanctions thus create more persistent cooperation norms. The study does not report group payoff or efficiency, but the contribution results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency effects in similar game designs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that both financial and social punishment mechanisms increase cooperation in a standard PGG, and that the effect is larger for financial punishment while the mechanism is active. However, the persistence of cooperation after punishment is removed is greater for social sanctions. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment will be positive for both financial and social punishment, but the durability of the effect may depend on the type of punishment. The absence of direct efficiency or payoff reporting means that predictions must be inferred from contribution rates, but the mapping is straightforward in standard PGGs. The paper's design and results are highly informative for settings with 4 players, continuous contributions, no communication, and known MPCR.

- source: Schroeder, K. B., Nettle, D., & McElreath, R. (2015). Interactions between personality and institutions in cooperative behaviour in humans. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that the opportunity for decentralized punishment in a repeated public goods game increases contributions to the public good compared to a no-punishment baseline. This effect is especially pronounced for individuals high in extraversion, who tend to free-ride in the absence of punishment but increase their contributions when punishment is possible. Agreeableness is associated with higher initial contributions regardless of institution, while neuroticism is associated with lower contributions in both conditions. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the increase in contributions with punishment suggests a likely increase in efficiency, subject to the cost of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a repeated public goods game increases contributions, particularly among certain personality types (notably extraverts). However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be quantified from this paper alone. The game design dimensions are well specified, and the punishment mechanism is standard (costly, decentralized, 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio). The findings support the general prediction that punishment increases cooperation, but do not allow direct prediction of efficiency outcomes for the downstream task.

- source: Engel, C., & Kurschilgen, M. (2013). The Coevolution of Behavior and Normative Expectations: An Experiment. *American Law and Economics Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that (1) both norm elicitation and legal framing increase cooperation in a repeated public goods game, but do not differ significantly in the absence of punishment; (2) introducing a costly peer punishment option (1:1 cost-to-impact) increases cooperation and reduces free-riding, but the effect is strongest when the norm is embedded in a legal frame; (3) in the presence of punishment, the legal frame prevents the crowding out of intrinsic motivation and leads to higher contributions and more stable norm perceptions; (4) average earnings are higher in the Law-S (legal frame with sanctions) treatment than in other sanctioning treatments, indicating that punishment can improve group welfare when combined with legal framing. However, the paper does not directly analyze efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, but average earnings per treatment are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with 4 players, 30 rounds, and a 1:1 punishment cost-to-impact ratio, enabling punishment increases group earnings and cooperation, especially when the norm is legally framed. The effect of punishment on efficiency is positive and most pronounced when combined with a legal frame. The control (no-punishment) efficiency is lower than in the punishment-enabled treatments, particularly Law-S. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment will increase efficiency, but the magnitude depends on the presence of legal/normative framing. The results are most directly applicable to games with continuous contributions, no chat, full information about others' contributions, and a 1:1 punishment cost-to-impact ratio.

- source: Visser, M., & Burns, J. (2015). Inequality, social sanctions and cooperation within South African fishing communities. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Punishment opportunities increase contribution rates in both equal and unequal endowment groups, with a larger effect in unequal groups. Low endowment players contribute a higher share of their endowment than high endowment players. Punishment is used less in unequal groups, but is more effective at raising contributions. Demand for punishment does not differ significantly between high and low endowment players, despite higher relative costs for low endowment players. Punishment is mainly targeted at free-riders, with antisocial punishment more common in equal groups. Socio-economic status and social attitudes (e.g., distrust in formal institutions) influence punishment behavior. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that peer punishment increases contribution rates in linear public goods games, especially in groups with unequal endowments. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified, and the findings about contribution rates and punishment behavior are informative for predicting behavioral responses to punishment. For efficiency prediction, the paper suggests that punishment is likely to increase efficiency in similar settings, especially in unequal groups, but the magnitude cannot be inferred. The absence of payoff/efficiency data is a key limitation for direct prediction.

- source: Vollan, B., Landmann, A., Zhou, Y. X., Hu, B. L., & Herrmann-Pillath, C. (2017). Cooperation and authoritarian values: An experimental study in China. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a one-shot, linear public goods game with a mild, non-deterrent punishment for under-contribution, exogenously imposed punishment rules (authoritarian/top-down) significantly increase average contributions compared to no law or endogenously chosen (democratic) punishment rules. This effect is strongest among participants (especially workers) with high deference to authority, and weaker or absent among those with low deference to authority (especially students). The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but since the game is linear and contributions are the main outcome, higher contributions imply higher efficiency. There is no evidence of a democracy premium in this Chinese sample; instead, authoritarian implementation is more effective at increasing cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a linear PGG with a mild, non-deterrent punishment, enabling punishment via exogenous (authoritarian) imposition increases average contributions (and thus efficiency) compared to no punishment. The effect is context-dependent: in this Chinese sample, exogenous punishment is more effective than democratic (endogenous) punishment, especially among those with high deference to authority. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on cultural context and the method of rule implementation (exogenous vs. endogenous). However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so predictions must be inferred from contribution rates.

- source: Bicskei, M., Lankau, M., & Bizer, K. (2016). Negative reciprocity and its relation to anger-like emotions in identity-homogeneous and -heterogeneous groups. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that when peer punishment is available in a one-shot PGG, the likelihood and intensity of punishment for low contributions is significantly lower in identity-homogeneous (in-group) groups than in identity-heterogeneous (out-group) or random groups. Anger-like emotions (anger, contempt, irritation) are a strong driver of punishment, but their effect is moderated by group identity: they lead to more punishment in out-groups than in-groups. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but argues that higher punishment in out-groups, being costly, likely reduces social welfare relative to in-groups, where punishment is less frequent and less intense.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on behavior in PGGs is moderated by group identity: punishment is less frequent and less intense in in-groups, and anger-like emotions drive punishment more in out-groups. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that in settings with identity-heterogeneous groups, punishment is more likely to be used, which (given the costliness of punishment) may reduce efficiency relative to in-groups. For prediction tasks, this paper is most informative about the behavioral mechanisms and moderators of punishment, but not about the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Engel, C., Beckenkamp, M., Glöckner, A., Irlenbusch, B., Hennig-Schmidt, H., Kube, S., Kurschilgen, M., Morell, A., Nicklisch, A., Normann, H. T., & Towfigh, E. (2014). First impressions are more important than early intervention: Qualifying broken windows theory in the lab. *International Review of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In repeated public goods games with and without punishment, the authors find that first impressions (i.e., the initial level of cooperation in the group) have a strong and lasting effect on subsequent contributions. The opportunity to punish freeriders (costly punishment) stabilizes cooperation and slows the decay of contributions over time, compared to games without punishment. However, the effect of early punishment (punishing in the first round) is small and fades over time, especially when punished players can retaliate. The main driver of sustained cooperation is good first impressions, with punishment playing a secondary, stabilizing role. The paper does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoffs, but higher contributions in the punishment condition imply higher efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in standard linear public goods games, enabling punishment increases and stabilizes contributions compared to no-punishment baselines, especially when initial group behavior is cooperative. While the paper does not report efficiency directly, the close link between contribution rate and efficiency in this design means the results are highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes. The effect of punishment is positive but depends on initial conditions; early punishment per se is less important than the overall opportunity for punishment. The findings are most applicable to 4-player, 10-round, continuous-contribution PGGs with standard punishment technology and no communication.

- source: Bell, A., Zhang, W., & Nou, K. (2016). Pesticide use and cooperative management of natural enemy habitat in a framed field experiment. *Agricultural Systems*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment uses a one-shot public goods game with three treatments: (1) Base (no punishment), (2) UCPun (conditional decision maker can be punished by an unconditional decision maker), and (3) CPun (conditional decision maker can punish an unconditional decision maker). The threat of punishment (UCPun) increases the slope of conditional cooperation but reduces the probability of being a conditional cooperator (more free riders). The power to punish (CPun) does not reduce conditional cooperation (no substitution effect on cooperation), but does lead to more sensitive punishment behavior. Responsibility effects (increased cooperation when given punishment power) are stronger than substitution effects. The overall effect of punishment on cooperation is positive, but the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how punishment mechanisms affect conditional cooperation in a one-shot public goods game with four players, MPCR 0.4, and costless punishment. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that enabling punishment increases the responsiveness of cooperators to others' contributions and may increase overall cooperation, but also increases free riding. For prediction tasks, the results imply that punishment is likely to increase cooperation-related behaviors, but the net effect on efficiency is ambiguous due to the rise in free riders. The absence of direct efficiency analysis limits the ability to quantitatively predict treatment efficiency from control efficiency, but the behavioral mechanisms identified are informative for modeling.

- source: Cheung, S. L. (2014). New insights into conditional cooperation and punishment from a strategy method experiment. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that both conditional cooperation and punishment in a public goods game are sensitive to the full distribution of others' contributions, not just the average. When punishment is available, more subjects contribute and a substantial fraction are willing to punish, with punishment increasing as the target's contribution falls below the punisher's and as the third player's contribution rises. However, the study does not directly report or analyze group efficiency, welfare, or total earnings as a function of punishment availability. The main findings are about the behavioral patterns of contribution and punishment, and the heterogeneity of types (conditional cooperators, free-riders, others).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how punishment affects contribution and punishment behavior in a one-shot, three-player public goods game with a strategy method. However, it does not directly report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does support the qualitative expectation that punishment increases contributions and that the effect depends on the distribution of others' contributions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is informative about mechanisms and behavioral responses, but not about the magnitude of efficiency changes.

- source: Thöni, C. (2014). Inequality aversion and antisocial punishment. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that, while inequality aversion can theoretically motivate antisocial punishment under certain conditions, empirical data from several public goods game experiments indicate that most antisocial punishment acts do not fit this explanation. Instead, antisocial punishment is often carried out by the richest group members and is not typically accompanied by punishment of free riders, contrary to the predictions of the inequality aversion model. The findings suggest that other motives, such as revenge or a desire to harm others, are more likely explanations for antisocial punishment. The paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, but notes that antisocial punishment is detrimental to the efficiency of informal punishment mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms behind antisocial punishment in public goods games with punishment, but it does not provide direct quantitative evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. It does, however, reinforce the point that the presence and frequency of antisocial punishment can undermine the positive effects of punishment on cooperation and efficiency, especially in environments where antisocial punishment is prevalent. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that game design features or subject pool characteristics that foster antisocial punishment may reduce or even reverse the efficiency gains from enabling punishment. However, the paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes directly, so it cannot be used to calibrate quantitative predictions of efficiency changes.

- source: Krasnow, M. M., Delton, A. W., Cosmides, L., & Tooby, J. (2015). Group Cooperation without Group Selection: Modest Punishment Can Recruit Much Cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, default_contrib, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The simulations demonstrate that when punishment is possible and can recruit labor (i.e., induce free riders to cooperate in the future), even modest levels of punishment are sufficient to sustain high levels of cooperation in public goods games. The model relaxes several assumptions of prior models, allowing for probabilistic punishment, continuous cooperation probabilities, individual recognition, and group mixing. The results show that both punishment and cooperation can evolve by individual selection, even in large groups, and that second-order free riders do not prevent the evolution of cooperation. However, the outcomes reported are behavioral (cooperation and punishment rates), not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment in public goods games can substantially increase cooperation rates, especially when punishment is probabilistic and can recruit labor. The findings suggest that game designs allowing for individual recognition and repeated interactions (mixing ecologies) will see higher cooperation supported by lower punishment rates. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. Its value is in supporting the mechanism by which punishment increases cooperation, which is likely to translate into higher efficiency in similar empirical designs, but this is not directly quantified here.

- source: Kamei, K. (2014). Conditional punishment. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment finds that individuals' punishment of non-cooperators increases with the amount of punishment assigned by other group members. Most subjects are 'free-riders' who do not punish, but about 23% are conditional punishers who increase punishment as others do. There is also a distinction between pro-social and anti-social conditional punishers. The study provides evidence that conditional punishment types are heterogeneous and that these types can predict unconditional punishment behavior. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed behavioral evidence on the heterogeneity and conditionality of punishment in a standard PGG with punishment, but does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. It is useful for understanding the mechanisms and types of punishment behavior that may underlie efficiency effects in similar games, but cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, suggesting that the presence of conditional punishers may amplify punishment effects when others also punish, but the net effect on efficiency is not measured.

- source: Li, Y. X., & Shen, B. (2013). The coevolution of partner switching and strategy updating in non-excludable public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in non-excludable public goods games, the ability to switch partners asymmetrically (i.e., only being able to sever outgoing ties) reduces the equilibrium level of cooperation compared to excludable (symmetric) PGGs. The addition of costly punishment, where cooperators pay a cost to reduce defectors' payoffs, substantially increases the equilibrium fraction of cooperators. The effect of punishment intensity (amplification factor) is also positive for cooperation. However, all outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that costly punishment increases cooperation rates in non-excludable PGGs with dynamic networks, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher cooperation rates typically imply higher efficiency, but the paper does not quantify this relationship or report actual payoffs. The model details (e.g., group size, network structure, punishment cost and effectiveness) are described, but the absence of payoff-based outcomes limits direct applicability to efficiency prediction.

- source: Shimao, H., & Nakamaru, M. (2013). Strict or Graduated Punishment? Effect of Punishment Strictness on the Evolution of Cooperation in Continuous Public Goods Games. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the effectiveness of punishment strictness in promoting cooperation depends on the population structure. In spatially structured populations (lattice), strict punishment (high strictness parameter) leads to higher evolved cooperation levels than graduated punishment. In random-matching (well-mixed) populations, graduated punishment (low strictness parameter) is more effective at increasing cooperation. The results are robust across a range of parameters for public goods efficiency and punishment effectiveness. However, the paper does not report direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the average cooperation level as the main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, efficiency) is moderated by the population structure: strict punishment is more effective in spatially structured settings, while graduated punishment is more effective in well-mixed settings. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the design dimension corresponding to population structure (e.g., fixed groups vs. random rematching) is a key moderator of punishment's effect. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its findings should be interpreted as informative about contribution/cooperation rates, which are closely related but not identical to efficiency.

- source: Hintze, A., & Adami, C. (2015). Punishment in public goods games leads to meta-stable phase transitions and hysteresis. *Physical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing punishment in public goods games shifts the critical synergy parameter (r) required for cooperation to emerge to lower values, making cooperation possible under less favorable conditions. Punishment acts as a catalyst for cooperation, even if it is used sparingly, and can stabilize cooperation below the critical point. However, punishment can also stabilize defection above the critical point if defectors reach a critical density. The system exhibits meta-stable phase transitions and hysteresis: once cooperation is established, it can persist even as conditions worsen, and vice versa. The main reported outcomes are the probabilities of cooperation and punishment, and the critical values of the synergy parameter, not direct efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the presence and parameters of punishment (cost, effectiveness) in a public goods game can substantially lower the threshold (synergy r) at which cooperation emerges. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the probability of cooperation is a strong proxy for efficiency in this context, as full cooperation would maximize group payoff. The findings suggest that, for a given game design, enabling punishment (especially if it is effective and not too costly) will increase the likelihood of high cooperation and thus higher efficiency, particularly near the critical synergy threshold. However, the paper does not provide quantitative efficiency values, so its support is indirect and best used for qualitative prediction or mechanistic understanding.

- source: Li, K., Cong, R., & Wang, L. (2016). Cooperation induced by random sequential exclusion. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the random sequential exclusion (R) mechanism for social exclusion in public goods games can sustain higher levels of cooperation (measured as the stationary frequency of cooperators and excluders) than the standard peer exclusion (P) mechanism, especially under adverse conditions such as high exclusion cost, low exclusion success probability, large group size, and low synergy factor. The advantage of the R mechanism is lost if the scheduling cost for coordination among excluders becomes too high. The results hold in both well-mixed and spatially structured populations. However, the paper does not report direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff, so the findings are about the prevalence of cooperative strategies rather than explicit welfare or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that more efficient or coordinated punishment mechanisms (like random sequential exclusion) can increase the prevalence of cooperative strategies in public goods games, especially when punishment costs are high or group size is large. However, because the paper does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Instead, it suggests that under certain design dimensions (low scheduling cost, high group size, high exclusion cost), mechanisms that reduce redundant punishment may be more effective at sustaining cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the structure and cost of punishment mechanisms are important moderators, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Quan, J., Liu, W., Chu, Y. Q., & Wang, X. J. (2017). Stochastic evolutionary voluntary public goods game with punishment in a Quasi-birth-and-death process. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment and non-participation strategies into a stochastic evolutionary public goods game changes the evolutionary dynamics and the likelihood of the system reaching cooperative, defecting, or non-participation equilibria. Higher punishment probability (alpha) and lower punishment cost (gamma) both increase the parameter regions where the system is likely to reach cooperative/punishing (C+P) equilibria. However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff; instead, it reports the probability of the system selecting each type of equilibrium as a function of the parameters. The results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under a wide range of conditions, but the effect is moderated by the cost and probability of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: it shows that the likelihood of cooperative/punishing equilibria increases with higher punishment probability and lower punishment cost, and that these effects are sensitive to the marginal per-capita return (r/N) and the fixed income for non-participants. However, because the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, its findings must be mapped from the probability of cooperative equilibria to expected efficiency. The results suggest that, in parameter regions where the C+P equilibrium is likely, efficiency would be higher than in regions dominated by defection or non-participation. Thus, the paper supports the idea that punishment can increase efficiency, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Cong, R., Zhao, Q. C., Li, K., & Wang, L. (2017). Individual mobility promotes punishment in evolutionary public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the optimal mobility (migration tendency) of punishers depends on the cost of punishment. For high punishment costs, a medium tendency to migrate (rather than always punishing or always running) maximizes the prevalence of punishers. For low punishment costs, always punishing is optimal. The presence of cooperators (second-order free-riders) changes the dynamics: in harsh environments (low enhancement factor), punishers benefit from moderate migration, but in more favorable environments, cooperators can dominate, and the advantage of punishment diminishes. The model shows that mobility can help punishers survive, but only under certain cost and environmental conditions. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but higher prevalence of punishers and cooperators is interpreted as beneficial for cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for how punishment, its cost, and the mobility of punishers affect the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under certain conditions, especially when the cost is not too high and punishers have a moderate migration tendency. For prediction tasks, the findings imply that the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive when punishment is not too costly and when the game environment allows for spatial structure and mobility. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the precision of quantitative predictions.

- source: Strang, S., & Park, S. Q. (2017). Human Cooperation and Its Underlying Mechanisms. *Social Behavior from Rodents to Humans: Neural Foundations and Clinical Implications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper synthesizes evidence that the threat or presence of punishment in public goods games and similar economic games leads to higher and more stable cooperation rates. It highlights that when punishment is available, cooperation is maintained, and when it is removed, cooperation declines. The review also discusses the emotional and neural correlates of cooperation and punishment, such as the role of negative emotions in driving punishment and the involvement of brain regions like the insula, striatum, and DLPFC. However, the paper does not provide quantitative or direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze how game design dimensions moderate these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong qualitative support for the idea that punishment increases cooperation in public goods games and related settings, and that this effect is robust across cultures and contexts. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates for the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it analyze how specific game design dimensions (such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost) moderate these effects. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is best used as background for the general mechanism that punishment increases cooperation, but not for precise quantitative prediction or for understanding the impact of specific design features.

- source: Dugar, S. (2013). NON-MONETARY INCENTIVES AND OPPORTUNISTIC BEHAVIOR: EVIDENCE FROM A LABORATORY PUBLIC GOOD GAME. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with groups of four, the introduction of non-monetary, costless disapproval points (social sanctions) after each round leads to significantly higher group contributions and group earnings (efficiency) compared to a baseline with no such mechanism. Approval points (social rewards) alone have a small, weakly significant effect on efficiency. The combination of approval and disapproval points produces the highest efficiency, with group earnings and contributions both significantly higher than in any other treatment. The effect is robust over 15 rounds, though some decay is observed. The study demonstrates that even costless, payoff-irrelevant social sanctions can substantially improve group welfare in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a costless, non-monetary punishment mechanism (disapproval points) in a standard linear public goods game increases efficiency (group earnings) relative to a baseline with no such mechanism. The effect is larger than that of a costless reward mechanism (approval points), and the combination of both is most effective. Because the punishment is costless and does not reduce group payoffs, all efficiency gains are realized. For prediction tasks, this suggests that introducing a non-monetary, costless punishment mechanism in a repeated, anonymous, four-player, 15-round linear PGG with MPCR=0.4 will increase efficiency substantially over the control. The effect is likely to be even larger if both approval and disapproval are available. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with costless, anonymous, non-monetary sanctions, and may not generalize to costly or monetary punishment.

- source: Peeters, R., & Vorsatz, M. (2013). IMMATERIAL REWARDS AND SANCTIONS IN A VOLUNTARY CONTRIBUTION EXPERIMENT. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing costless, immaterial feedback (smilies/frownies) does not significantly increase average group contributions or payoffs in a voluntary contribution public goods game. Under partner matching, feedback treatments show higher variance in group contributions, with some groups sustaining high contributions, but the average effect is not significant. Under stranger matching, feedback treatments do not outperform control and may even reduce contributions. The effect of feedback on future contributions is context-dependent: under partner matching, sending/receiving emoticons can reduce future contributions; under stranger matching, receiving a frowny can increase future contributions for some participants. Overall, immaterial feedback does not reliably improve efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that costless, immaterial punishment or reward mechanisms (such as emoticons) do not significantly improve efficiency or group payoff in standard public goods games, regardless of matching protocol. For prediction tasks, the presence of immaterial, non-costly, non-monetary punishment should not be expected to increase efficiency relative to control. The effect of such feedback is highly variable and context-dependent, with no robust positive effect on group payoff or efficiency. The findings suggest that, unless punishment or reward is material (i.e., has real payoff consequences), enabling such mechanisms is unlikely to improve efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Galbiati, R., & Vertova, P. (2014). How laws affect behavior: Obligations, incentives and cooperative behavior. *International Review of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Obligations (minimum required contributions) significantly increase average contributions to the public good, even in the absence of incentives. Non-binding incentives (probabilistic, low-stakes rewards/punishments) alone do not affect contributions, but when combined with obligations, they substantially increase contributions beyond the effect of obligations alone. The effect of obligations and incentives is complementary. The mechanisms are traced to changes in beliefs about others' contributions and in individuals' willingness to cooperate. The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the design implies that higher contributions increase group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a one-shot linear public good game with 6 players and no communication, introducing a minimum contribution requirement (obligation) increases average contributions, and adding even weak, non-binding incentives (probabilistic punishment/reward) further increases contributions. While the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the structure of the game means that higher contributions correspond to higher efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence of obligations and (even weak) punishment/reward mechanisms can be expected to increase efficiency relative to a baseline with neither, especially when both are present. However, the absence of direct efficiency data means that quantitative predictions must be inferred from the reported contribution rates.

- source: Nakamaru, M., & Yokoyama, A. (2014). The Effect of Ostracism and Optional Participation on the Evolution of Cooperation in the Voluntary Public Goods Game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in voluntary public goods games, allowing group members to exclude (ostracize) candidates with low reputation (defectors) leads to the evolution and maintenance of high cooperation rates. Optional participation (where individuals can choose whether to join a group based on group reputation) does not sustain cooperation. The strictest exclusion rule (requiring unanimous acceptance for membership) is most effective at promoting cooperation. The model is evolutionary and simulation-based, and outcomes are reported as the proportion of cooperators, not as group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that costless ostracism (exclusion of defectors) is a highly effective mechanism for sustaining cooperation in public goods games, more so than optional participation alone. While the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the implication is that group efficiency would be higher when ostracism is enabled, since the share of cooperators is much higher. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is costless and takes the form of exclusion, rather than costly point-based punishment. The results are less directly applicable to predicting the quantitative effect of standard costly punishment on efficiency, but they support the qualitative prediction that effective exclusion or ostracism can greatly increase group efficiency if measured.

- source: Gangadharan, L., Nikiforakis, N., & Villeval, M. C. (2017). Normative conflict and the limits of self-governance in heterogeneous populations. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: Allowing communication and rewards in a public goods game leads to near-maximal efficiency in homogeneous groups, but only partial efficiency in heterogeneous groups where returns from the public good differ. In homogeneous groups, communication increases group earnings to 94% of the maximum, while in heterogeneous groups, the increase is only 80% of the maximum. The main reason is that most heterogeneous groups prioritize equality or compromise between equality and efficiency, rather than maximizing group earnings. Rewards are not systematically used to offset inequality. The loss in efficiency in heterogeneous groups is due both to lower contributions and to under-use of rewards. The study demonstrates that normative conflict can limit the effectiveness of self-governance mechanisms in promoting efficiency in heterogeneous populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the efficiency gains from enabling communication and rewards in public goods games depend critically on group homogeneity. In homogeneous groups, such mechanisms can achieve near-maximal efficiency. In heterogeneous groups, even with communication and rewards, efficiency gains are much smaller due to normative conflict between equality and efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of heterogeneity in returns (mpcr differences) is a strong moderator that limits the efficiency benefit of enabling communication and rewards. The absence of punishment means the results are most directly applicable to reward-based or communication-based interventions, but the findings on normative conflict are likely relevant for predicting the limits of punishment as well, especially in heterogeneous settings.

- source: Kurokawa, S., & Ihara, Y. (2017). Evolution of group-wise cooperation: Is direct reciprocity insufficient?. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper shows that in n-player repeated prisoners' dilemma games with non-rivalrous public goods, direct reciprocity alone can support the evolution of cooperation (and thus high group efficiency), especially as group size increases. This holds even when individuals sometimes make mistakes or have imperfect information, provided they use sufficiently optimistic conditional strategies. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcome is the condition under which cooperative strategies are selectively favored over defection, which is directly tied to group payoff and efficiency. The paper does not provide empirical or simulated efficiency values, but gives analytic thresholds for when cooperation is favored.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games with non-rivalrous benefits, increasing group size can make cooperation (and thus high efficiency) more likely, even in the presence of mistakes or imperfect information, as long as players use optimistic conditional strategies. However, the paper does not model punishment, so it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. Its results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions as a function of group size, information, and error, but not for the effect of punishment. The paper is useful for understanding baseline efficiency in repeated public goods games with direct reciprocity, but not for predicting the incremental effect of punishment.

- source: Nosenzo, D., & Tufano, F. (2017). The effect of voluntary participation on cooperation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment compares three main treatments: Baseline (forced participation), Unconditional (voluntary participation before knowing partner's contribution), and Conditional (voluntary participation after seeing partner's contribution). In Baseline and Unconditional, average contributions and efficiency are nearly identical (about 22% of endowment contributed; efficiency ~22%). In Conditional, average contributions and efficiency are much higher (47% of endowment; efficiency up to 81.9% among those who agree to play, and 46.6% overall). The positive effect of voluntary participation on efficiency is only observed when participation can be used as a threat against free-riding. There is little evidence that unconditional voluntary participation leads to assortative matching of cooperators, even when the outside option is increased. The main mechanism for increased efficiency is the threat of non-participation disciplining free-riders, not selection. However, frequent withdrawals can reduce realized efficiency gains.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-person one-shot PGG, enabling voluntary participation only increases efficiency if players can condition their participation on knowledge of the partner's intended contribution (i.e., if non-participation is a credible threat against free-riding). Unconditional voluntary participation (without information) does not improve efficiency, even with a higher outside option. For prediction, the presence of a conditional participation mechanism (a form of endogenous partner exclusion or exit threat) is a strong moderator of punishment-like effects on efficiency, even in the absence of explicit costly punishment. The results suggest that, in similar game designs, enabling conditional exit can substitute for explicit punishment in raising efficiency, but unconditional exit does not. Control efficiency (forced participation) is a good predictor of efficiency in unconditional exit games, but not in conditional exit games, where efficiency is much higher.

- source: Smaldino, P. E., & Lubell, M. (2014). Institutions and Cooperation in an Ecology of Games. *Artificial Life*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model demonstrates that reputational exclusion, when information about behavior is reliable and flows quickly, allows cooperators to achieve much higher payoffs relative to defectors than capacity constraints. However, if reputation is slow to converge or cooperators are rare, capacity constraints are more effective. The ability of cooperators to join many games under reputation is key to their advantage. The model also finds that increasing the productivity of the public goods game (higher multiplier) increases the relative payoff to cooperators under reputation, but not necessarily under capacity constraints. Limiting the number of games an agent can play reduces the effectiveness of reputation-based exclusion. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning (costly punishment), so direct evidence on punishment is not provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it models only exclusion-based institutions (capacity constraints and reputation-based entry) rather than costly punishment. However, it is highly informative about the effects of group entry rules and reputation mechanisms on efficiency and cooperation. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is only adjacent, but it provides strong evidence that exclusion and reputation can be powerful alternatives to punishment for promoting efficiency, especially when information is reliable and cooperators are common. The findings suggest that the design of group entry and information flow can be as important as punishment in determining efficiency outcomes.

- source: Stewart, A. J., Parsons, T. L., & Plotkin, J. B. (2016). Evolutionary consequences of behavioral diversity. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in iterated public goods games, increasing the number of available contribution levels (behavioral diversity) can either increase or decrease group efficiency, depending on the ratio of public benefit to individual cost (mpcr). With low mpcr, more choices allow for more robust cooperative strategies and higher mean payoffs. With high mpcr, the abundance of suboptimal robust strategies causes populations to get stuck at lower efficiency. The ability to punish (by reducing one's own contribution in response to defection) is necessary for two-choice strategies to be robust, but explicit costly punishment is not modeled. The paper provides analytic conditions for when simple strategies can resist invasion by more complex ones, and uses evolutionary simulations to show the impact of choice set size on mean payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games as a function of the number of contribution options (all_or_nothing, player_count, mpcr, etc.), but it does not model explicit costly punishment as a separate mechanism. Instead, 'punishment' is operationalized as the ability to reduce one's own contribution in response to others' defection. The findings suggest that increasing the number of contribution options (behavioral diversity) can either help or hurt efficiency, depending on mpcr. For prediction, the paper supports the idea that more behavioral options do not always increase efficiency, and that the effect depends on the underlying game parameters. However, because explicit costly punishment is not present, the results are only adjacent to the core prediction task regarding punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled games.

- source: Zhang, J. L., Zhang, C. Y., & Cao, M. (2015). How insurance affects altruistic provision in threshold public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing insurance for contributors in threshold public goods games increases the provision of the public good and can improve the efficiency (group payoff) of the system. The effect is strongest when the insurance compensation is high and the required threshold for provision is high. The model shows that insurance can shift the evolutionary dynamics to favor contributors over defectors, depending on parameter values. The allocation rule for surplus contributions (parameter omega) also moderates the effect. The paper does not analyze punishment or sanctions, but provides a theoretical basis for how risk-mitigation mechanisms (like insurance) can promote cooperation and efficiency in threshold public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that risk-mitigation mechanisms (insurance) for contributors can increase efficiency in threshold public goods games, especially when the risk of loss is high and insurance compensation is generous. While it does not study punishment, it is relevant for predicting efficiency in games where contributors face risk and can be protected from loss. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, adding insurance or similar mechanisms can substitute for punishment in promoting efficiency. The model is most applicable to threshold public goods games with all-or-nothing contributions, no communication, and no explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The results are parameter-dependent and highlight the importance of the threshold, insurance compensation, and allocation rules for surplus contributions.

- source: Moreira, J. A., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2013). Evolution of collective action in adaptive social structures. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can adapt their social ties—specifically, break links with defectors more quickly than with cooperators—cooperation can be sustained at higher levels than in well-mixed or static networks. The evolutionary dynamics shift from defection dominance to a scenario with stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors, depending on the group size, enhancement factor, and network adaptation rates. Stochastic effects (e.g., mutation, exploration) further help populations escape defection traps and maintain cooperation. However, the paper does not analyze the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it report group efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding how endogenous partner selection and adaptive network structure can promote cooperation in PGGs, which may indirectly inform predictions about efficiency in environments where such mechanisms are present. However, it does not address punishment or sanctions, nor does it provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that adaptive partner selection can increase cooperation rates, but it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment or other interventions.

- source: Riedel, N., & Schildberg-Hörisch, H. (2013). Asymmetric obligations. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that both high and low minimum contribution obligations (even when only weakly enforced) increase contributions in the initial periods compared to no obligation, but this effect is not sustained in the long run. Asymmetric obligations are as effective as symmetric ones: for a given own obligation, the partner's obligation does not affect behavior. The effect of obligations is interpreted as arising from an emotional cost of disobeying, not from expectations about others' behavior. No direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that weakly enforced minimum contribution obligations can temporarily increase contributions in public goods games, regardless of whether obligations are symmetric or asymmetric. However, since the study does not include a standard punishment institution (where players can assign costly punishment to others), and the 'punishment' here is a very weak, probabilistic, externally imposed fine for not meeting a minimum, the results are only adjacent to the prediction task. The findings suggest that expressive law (weak obligations) can have a short-term effect on cooperation, but do not inform about the efficiency effects of endogenous peer punishment. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, etc.) are well specified, but the absence of a standard punishment treatment limits direct applicability to the prediction of efficiency under peer punishment.

- source: Hauser, O. P., Hendriks, A., Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2016). Think global, act local: Preserving the global commons. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that when participants can observe their neighbors' contributions to the public good and can condition their pairwise cooperation (in a PD) on this information, contributions to the public good are sustained at high levels, even in very large groups (up to 1,000 participants). In the control condition, where such information is not available, contributions decline over time. The mechanism is not direct punishment but rather the withholding of cooperation in a separate pairwise game, which acts as a social sanction. The effect is robust to group size and does not require participants to know the overall level of public good provision. The main outcome is sustained contribution, not measured efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mechanisms allowing for targeted social sanctions (withholding cooperation in a pairwise PD) based on observed public good contributions can sustain high contribution rates in large groups. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to punishment (withholding cooperation as a sanction) but does not involve costly punishment or explicit payoff reduction. The findings suggest that enabling targeted, observable social sanctions—even if not costly—can have a strong positive effect on contribution rates, which may translate to higher efficiency in similar designs, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Zhang, H. R., & Perc, M. (2016). Evolution of conditional cooperation under multilevel selection. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in public goods games with continuous contribution and multilevel selection, the presence of conditional strategies (modeled as piecewise linear response functions) enables the emergence and stability of high cooperation levels. The dominant strategy is robust to initial conditions and parameter changes. The study does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on average contribution and strategy prevalence.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that conditional cooperation strategies can sustain high contribution levels in public goods games under multilevel selection, even without punishment or reward. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment-related dimensions. Its findings are most relevant for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of explicit sanctioning mechanisms, and for interpreting the role of conditional strategies as an alternative to punishment. It is not directly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment.

- source: Greiff, M., & Paetzel, F. (2016). Second-order beliefs in reputation systems with endogenous evaluations - an experimental study. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a repeated two-player public good game, providing information about both one's own and the partner's evaluation (reputation) increases contributions compared to control and to the treatment where only the partner's evaluation is shown. When only the partner's evaluation is available, contributions do not increase significantly relative to control. The effect is explained by the role of second-order beliefs in conditional cooperation. The study does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but higher contributions in the BOTH-INFO treatment imply higher group payoffs. There is no punishment or costly sanctioning; evaluations are costless, non-binding signals.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for predicting the effect of reputation-based information (not costly punishment) on contribution rates in repeated two-player public good games. It suggests that simply providing information about others' evaluations is insufficient to increase cooperation, but providing both own and partner's evaluation can increase contributions. However, because the study does not include a costly punishment mechanism, its direct relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment is limited. The findings are most relevant for settings where social image or reputation feedback (not material punishment) is the main intervention. For prediction tasks involving costly punishment, this paper provides only adjacent evidence.

- source: Zhou, T. W., Ding, S., Fan, W. J., & Wang, H. (2016). An improved public goods game model with reputation effect on the spatial lattices. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that embedding a reputation effect into the utility function of agents in a spatial PGG model leads to a substantial increase in the stationary fraction of cooperators, especially as the reputation factor increases. The critical synergy factor required for cooperation to emerge is reduced, and the region of parameter space supporting full cooperation is expanded. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are based on Monte Carlo simulations and are relevant for understanding how reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation, but do not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation in spatial PGGs, but does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. For the downstream prediction task—predicting efficiency effects of punishment—this paper is only weakly informative. It suggests that non-monetary social mechanisms (like reputation) can increase cooperation, but does not provide data or analysis on how punishment affects efficiency, nor does it report group payoff or welfare. The game design dimensions are described in detail for the reputation mechanism, but not for punishment or efficiency.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2017). Publishing the donation list incompletely promotes the emergence of cooperation in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: Publishing the donation list incompletely (only for those who contribute above a threshold) leads to higher total contributions and more equal contributions among players in a public goods game on a network. There is an optimal threshold for publication that maximizes these effects. The per capita number of neighbors and the amount of historical data players consider also moderate the effect, but the main result is robust: partial transparency outperforms full transparency or secrecy. The model is agent-based and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, it relies on social reputation and face loss as motivators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that social information mechanisms (such as partial publication of contribution lists) can increase cooperation in public goods games, but it does not study monetary punishment or its effect on efficiency. The findings are relevant for predicting the effect of information disclosure or reputation-based interventions, not for predicting the effect of adding costly punishment. The sum of contributions is a proxy for group welfare, but efficiency (as defined by payoff relative to full cooperation) is not directly reported. Use this paper as informative for the effect of social/reputational incentives, not for monetary punishment treatments.

- source: Yang, Z. H., Li, Z., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2013). Effects of adaptive dynamical linking in networked games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that adaptive dynamical linking—where agents lengthen or shorten social ties based on whether their partners cooperate or defect—can dramatically increase the prevalence of cooperation in both the prisoner's dilemma and public goods game. The effect is stronger when agents adjust their links more rapidly (higher adjustment rate, Δ), and under weak selection. The mechanism does not involve any explicit punishment, reward, or reputation system. The outcome measured is the frequency of full cooperation (all-cooperator absorbing state) in repeated simulations. There is no direct measurement or discussion of group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task of how punishment affects efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant as a demonstration that network adaptation (partner switching) can promote cooperation, but it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.
