  findings: The experiments show that participants are more likely to reward cooperative behavior than to punish non-cooperative behavior, and that this tendency is stronger in public good dilemmas (where cooperation involves giving up private property) than in common resource dilemmas (where cooperation involves refraining from taking collective property). When given a choice, most participants prefer to reward rather than punish. The size of rewards is also larger than the size of punishments, especially in public good dilemmas. The study does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that the preference for reward over punishment may have implications for collective welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public good and common resource games. It shows that, when given a choice, people are more willing to reward than to punish, and that punishment is used less frequently and less intensely, especially in public good dilemmas. However, because the study does not measure efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that the mere availability of punishment may not lead to high usage, especially if reward is also available, and that the type of social dilemma (public good vs. common resource) moderates willingness to punish. For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment may be less effective at increasing efficiency in public good dilemmas if participants are reluctant to use it, particularly when reward is also an option.

- source: Diekmann, A., & Przepiorka, W. (2015). Punitive preferences, monetary incentives and tacit coordination in the punishment of defectors promote cooperation in humans. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when only one group member is needed to punish a defector (step-level public good), groups with punishment cost heterogeneity (one strong, low-cost punisher) achieve higher rates of punishment and lower rates of defection than symmetric groups. Tacit coordination on the strong punisher leads to more efficient punishment (i.e., punishment is more often carried out by only one group member). The presence of a penalty for defectors (i.e., punishment that also harms the defector) is necessary to reduce defection rates. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the behavioral results suggest that punishment cost heterogeneity can improve the effectiveness and efficiency of norm enforcement in such games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment cost heterogeneity (i.e., having a designated or focal punisher with lower costs) increases the effectiveness and efficiency of punishment in step-level public good games, as measured by behavioral outcomes (punishment and defection rates). While it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, the findings suggest that game designs enabling tacit coordination on a single, low-cost punisher are likely to yield higher efficiency when punishment is enabled, especially when a penalty for defectors is present. The results are most directly relevant for step-level or volunteer's dilemma variants of public goods games, rather than standard linear PGGs. The absence of explicit efficiency or payoff data limits direct quantitative prediction, but the qualitative pattern is clear: punishment is more effective and less wasteful when cost heterogeneity allows for coordination on a single punisher.

- source: Delton, A. W., Nemirow, J., Robertson, T. E., Cimino, A., & Cosmides, L. (2013). Merely Opting Out of a Public Good Is Moralized: An Error Management Approach to Cooperation. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that people who opt out of contributing to public goods are moralized and sometimes punished, even if they have not yet free ridden. This preventive moralization is stronger in public goods than in club goods, and is mediated by the perceived likelihood that the non-contributor will free ride in the future. The effect is robust across implicit, explicit, and behavioral measures, and is observed in both student and nationally representative adult samples. However, the studies do not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total earnings, and thus do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment and moralization are triggered by non-contribution in public goods contexts, even before actual free riding occurs. It demonstrates that costly punishment is more likely in public goods than in club goods, but does not report on group efficiency or total payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment mechanisms can increase the likelihood of punishment being used in public goods games, especially when free riding is possible. However, it does not provide direct evidence about how punishment affects group efficiency or welfare, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited to informing about the behavioral context in which punishment is likely to be applied.

- source: Gatiso, T. T., Vollan, B., & Nuppenau, E. A. (2015). Resource scarcity, and democratic elections in commons dilemmas: An experiment on forest use in Ethiopia. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that resource scarcity (lower initial stock) leads to less cooperative behavior (higher HR), and that the way sanctions are implemented matters: democratically elected sanctions increase cooperation (lower HR) both under abundance and scarcity, while imposed sanctions can reduce cooperation (higher HR) under abundance (crowding-out effect), but not under scarcity. The main outcome is behavioral (HR), not efficiency or group payoff. Theoretical efficiency levels are provided, but not empirically analyzed as outcomes. The findings suggest that participatory rule-making is more effective than top-down sanctions for sustaining cooperation, especially when resources are not extremely scarce.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how the method of sanction implementation (election vs. imposition) moderates the effect of punishment on cooperation in a common-pool resource game with real-world framing. However, because the main outcome is behavioral (harvest ratio) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results must be mapped with caution to efficiency prediction tasks. Theoretical efficiency levels are given, but empirical results focus on HR. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, no explicit MPCR, probabilistic punishment, no rewards, summary info shown, anonymous punishment) are well described, supporting mapping to prediction models. The main implication is that democratically elected sanctions are more likely to increase cooperation (and thus efficiency) than imposed sanctions, especially under resource abundance. Under scarcity, the difference is smaller, but elections still outperform imposition.

- source: Zhang, B. Y., & Hofbauer, J. (2016). Quantal response methods for equilibrium selection in 2 x 2 coordination games. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a mini public goods game with punishment, the QRE method selects a cooperative equilibrium if the punishment is sufficiently strong (i.e., if the fine-to-fee ratio is high enough). The selection of cooperation depends on the effectiveness and cost of punishment. For intermediate punishment, cooperation can dominate for some parameter values even if the limiting QRE is defection. The results are derived analytically and illustrated with theoretical examples, but no empirical or simulated efficiency or payoff data are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical conditions for when punishment will lead to the selection of a cooperative equilibrium in a 2x2 mini public goods game. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it implies that strong punishment can shift equilibrium selection from defection to cooperation, which would be expected to increase efficiency in a public goods context. The results are most directly relevant for games with two players, binary choices, and explicit punishment options, and can inform predictions about the qualitative effect of punishment strength and cost on equilibrium selection, but not quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Sugiarto, H. S., Chung, N. N., Lai, C. H., & Chew, L. Y. (2015). Socioecological regime shifts in the setting of complex social interactions. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that social ostracism (punishment) can maintain cooperation in a common-pool resource setting, but the resilience of cooperation and the occurrence of regime shifts depend strongly on the structure of the social network. Higher network degree (more social ties) increases the width of the hysteresis curve, making the system more resilient to regime shifts (sudden collapse of cooperation and resources). In sparse networks, regime shifts occur more easily and hysteresis is reduced or absent. The model shows that both social and ecological parameters (e.g., ostracism strength, opportunity cost, resource inflow) can drive regime shifts. However, the main outcomes are the fraction of cooperators and resource levels, not direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that punishment (ostracism) can stabilize cooperation in a public-goods-like environment, and that the effect of punishment is moderated by network structure (player connectivity). However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment is more effective at maintaining cooperation (and thus, by implication, higher group welfare) in denser networks, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits its predictive value for the target outcome.

- source: Xu, M., Zheng, D. F., Xu, C., Zhong, L. X., & Hui, P. M. (2015). Cooperative behavior in N-person evolutionary snowdrift games with punishment. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing costly punishment into an N-person snowdrift game (a close variant of the public goods game) can suppress non-cooperators and promote a cooperative population, but only under certain conditions: low cost-to-benefit ratio, large group size, and strong punishment effectiveness. In these cases, the system reaches a 'frozen' phase with only cooperators and punishers. If the punishment is weak, the cost-to-benefit ratio is high, or the group size is small, punishers are eliminated and the system remains a mix of cooperators and non-cooperators. The model is analyzed using replicator dynamics and confirmed by simulation. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the elimination of non-cooperators implies higher group performance in those parameter regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in N-person public-goods-like games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the steady-state elimination of non-cooperators in the presence of strong punishment, large group size, and low cost-to-benefit ratio suggests that efficiency would be higher in these regimes when punishment is enabled. Conversely, when punishment is weak, costly, or the group is small, punishment does not improve cooperation and likely does not improve efficiency. The findings are most relevant for settings where the game structure is similar to the N-person snowdrift game (cost shared among cooperators, task completed if at least one cooperator exists), which is close but not identical to the standard public goods game.

- source: Chierchia, G., Lesemann, F. H. P., Snower, D., Vogel, M., & Singer, T. (2017). Caring Cooperators and Powerful Punishers: Differential Effects of Induced Care and Power Motivation on Different Types of Economic Decision Making. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: Inducing a Care motive increases a latent cooperation factor (which includes public goods contributions), while Power induction increases a punishment factor (punishment in 2nd/3rd party punishment games, ultimatum, impunity). The two factors are largely orthogonal. Care increases cooperation relative to control, but does not increase punishment. Power increases punishment relative to Care, but not relative to control. No direct efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punishment and cooperation are distinct behavioral tendencies, and that motivational context (Care vs. Power) can differentially affect them. However, it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does suggest that punishment behavior can be increased by Power motives, but this is not linked to group payoff or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by clarifying that punishment and cooperation are separable and can be independently manipulated.

- source: Griffin, C., & Belmonte, A. (2017). Cyclic public goods games: Compensated coexistence among mutual cheaters stabilized by optimized penalty taxation. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a cyclic public goods game with three species, the system without penalty taxation is unstable and leads to the dominance of a single species, with the interior coexistence point being a point of minimal total population growth (i.e., lowest efficiency). Imposing a penalty tax (punishment) on population growth can stabilize the interior fixed point and allow for coexistence of all species, but this tax is not redistributed and thus reduces overall efficiency further. The optimal tax is the minimal one that achieves stability, but any positive tax reduces total population growth below the already minimal level at the coexistence point. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include empirical or simulation data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in a cyclic public goods game with three types of mutual cheaters, introducing a penalty tax (punishment) can stabilize coexistence but always reduces total population growth (efficiency) compared to the no-punishment case. The penalty is not redistributed, so it is a pure cost. Thus, in similar game designs, enabling punishment (as a non-redistributive tax) is predicted to decrease efficiency relative to the control. The findings are most relevant for settings with cyclic/multispecies structure and non-redistributive punishment, and less so for standard two-strategy PGGs or for punishment that is redistributed. The results are directly informative for predicting the direction of efficiency change (less positive) when enabling punishment in such models.

- source: Powers, S. T., & Lehmann, L. (2017). When is bigger better? The effects of group size on the evolution of helping behaviours. *Biological Reviews*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that as group size increases, the marginal benefits of helping, the effectiveness of reciprocity, and relatedness all tend to decrease, making cooperation less likely to evolve or be stable in large groups. Punishment can stabilize cooperation, but its effectiveness also declines with group size unless institutional mechanisms are in place. The authors argue that human societies have overcome these limitations through the evolution of institutions that coordinate sanctioning and information sharing, thereby enabling large-scale cooperation. The paper provides a conceptual framework for understanding how game design features (such as group size, punishment, and information structure) interact to affect the evolution and maintenance of cooperation, but does not provide quantitative predictions or new data on efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding the qualitative effects of group size and institutional punishment on cooperation and efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that, all else equal, increasing group size will tend to reduce the effectiveness of both reciprocity and punishment in sustaining cooperation, unless institutional mechanisms are present to coordinate sanctioning and information. The paper highlights the importance of considering diminishing marginal returns, monitoring costs, and second-order free-riding in large groups. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that punishment will be less effective at increasing efficiency in larger groups unless institutional features (e.g., centralized punishment, coordinated monitoring) are present. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates or experimental data for efficiency outcomes, so its support is primarily conceptual and mechanistic.

- source: Bshary, R., & Raihani, N. J. (2017). Helping in humans and other animals: a fruitful interdisciplinary dialogue. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper argues that mechanisms such as punishment and reward, studied extensively in human public goods games, are also relevant in non-human animal cooperation. It reviews how laboratory studies (often using public goods or n-player social dilemma games) have inspired comparative research, and how features like punishment, partner choice, and communication can affect cooperation rates. The authors emphasize the need for more ecologically valid experiments and for understanding how laboratory findings translate to real-world helping and cooperation. The review notes that while punishment and reward can increase cooperation in public goods games, most evidence is about behavioral outcomes, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and comparative context for how punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in public-goods-like games, but does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency effects. It is useful for understanding the mechanisms and moderators that may influence the effect of punishment on cooperation, but cannot be used to predict efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions or control efficiency. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of ecological validity, the role of communication, and the need to consider real-world payoff structures.

- source: Drouvelis, M., Nosenzo, D., & Sefton, M. (2017). Team incentives and leadership. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: Introducing a leader with discretionary reward power in a repeated team production game (a close variant of the public goods game) increases group efficiency from about 30-33% to 72-76% of the maximum possible. Leaders use their power to reward high contributors and withhold rewards from low contributors, effectively incentivizing effort. This effect holds for both homogeneous and heterogeneous teams. However, the distribution of gains is highly unequal, with leaders capturing most of the surplus. When workers can choose how much power to delegate to the leader, they typically restrict it to less than half the team output, and the potential efficiency gains are not fully realized. The main reason is that workers do not benefit much from leadership, as leaders only compensate them for effort costs and keep most of the residual output.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in VCM-like team production games, introducing a central authority with discretionary reward power (a form of positive sanctioning) can dramatically increase group efficiency, even when the leader is self-interested. The effect is robust to team heterogeneity. However, the efficiency gains are distributed unequally, and when delegation is endogenous, reluctance to empower the leader limits the realized gains. For prediction tasks, the presence of a leader with reward power (as opposed to equal sharing) should be expected to increase efficiency substantially, but the effect may be moderated if the leader's power is limited or if workers can restrict delegation. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where the 'punishment' dimension is implemented as positive reward power rather than costly punishment, but the mechanism (centralized sanctioning) is closely related.

- source: Croson, R., Fatas, E., Neugebauer, T., & Morales, A. J. (2015). Excludability: A laboratory study on forced ranking in team production. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that excludability (the ability to exclude the lowest contributor from group benefits) dramatically increases both contributions and efficiency in linear public goods (VCM) and best-shot (BSM) games, but not in weakest-link (WLM) games. In the VCM, efficiency rises from 65% (baseline) to 83% (excludability) and 99% (excludability with redistribution). In the BSM, efficiency rises from 81% (baseline) to 86% (excludability), but redistribution reduces efficiency to 75%. In the WLM, excludability alone does not improve efficiency (52% in both baseline and excludability), but redistribution increases efficiency to 72%. Thus, the effect of excludability on efficiency is highly dependent on the production function, and redistribution is necessary for efficiency gains in environments with strong complementarities (WLM).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment-like exclusion mechanisms on efficiency depends critically on the production function. In standard linear public goods games (VCM), enabling exclusion of the lowest contributor increases efficiency substantially, and redistribution of the excluded share further boosts efficiency. In weakest-link games, exclusion alone does not improve efficiency, but redistribution is necessary for efficiency gains. In best-shot games, exclusion increases efficiency, but redistribution actually reduces it. For prediction tasks, the production function (average, minimum, or maximum) is a key moderator of the efficiency effect of punishment/exclusion. Control efficiency is a strong predictor, but the treatment effect of exclusion is highly context-dependent.

- source: Vollan, B., Prediger, S., & Frölich, M. (2013). Co-managing common-pool resources: Do formal rules have to be adapted to traditional ecological norms?. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This field-lab experiment with communal farmers in southern Africa shows that the efficiency of formal, democratically chosen rules for managing a common-pool resource depends on their alignment with local ecological norms. The quota rule (limiting extraction) increases group earnings and resource availability in both high and low sustainability groups, but is unpopular. The rotation rule only increases efficiency in low sustainability groups (who previously failed to coordinate), but is ineffective or even counterproductive in high sustainability groups (who already tacitly coordinated for sustainability), due to high rates of rule breaking. The lottery rule is ineffective in both types of groups. Rule compliance is lower when rules conflict with local norms, and rule breaking is not mainly motivated by profit. Thus, the effect of rule-based interventions on efficiency is highly context-dependent.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of introducing a formal rule (akin to a punishment or restriction mechanism) on efficiency in a common-pool resource game, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect depends on the compatibility of the rule with pre-existing group norms. Rules that align with local sustainability norms (e.g., quotas in groups that value sustainability) can increase efficiency, while rules that conflict with these norms (e.g., rotation in high-norm groups) may reduce efficiency due to non-compliance. The presence of a sanctioning mechanism (imperfect monitoring and penalty) is not sufficient to ensure compliance or efficiency gains. Thus, the effect of punishment-like interventions on efficiency is moderated by group-level norms and prior behavior, not just by the formal game design. This paper is highly informative for settings where the main intervention is a formal rule or restriction, but less so for classic peer-punishment PGGs.

- source: Schonmann, R. H., & Boyd, R. (2016). A simple rule for the evolution of contingent cooperation in large groups. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that contingent cooperation in large groups can invade and persist if relatedness is above a threshold that depends sensitively on the threshold fraction of cooperators required for cooperation to be sustained. The required relatedness increases rapidly as the threshold for cooperation increases. The number of repeated interactions (iterations) required for cooperation to invade also increases rapidly with the threshold and decreases with relatedness. The model shows that, under realistic levels of relatedness, only strategies that require cooperation from a small fraction of the group can invade; strategies that require near-universal cooperation are unlikely to evolve. The paper argues that contingent punishment (not formally modeled here) is a more plausible mechanism for the evolution of widespread cooperation, because even a small fraction of punishers can induce high levels of cooperation. The results are supported by mathematical derivations and numerical simulations of evolutionary dynamics in group-structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how group size, relatedness, and the threshold for contingent cooperation affect the evolutionary success (and thus efficiency) of cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It does not directly model or analyze punishment, but its results suggest that punishment-based mechanisms are more likely to support high efficiency in large groups than pure contingent cooperation. For prediction tasks, the paper's formulas can be used to estimate the conditions under which cooperation (and thus high efficiency) can be sustained, given the group structure and behavioral rules. However, because the paper does not model explicit punishment or compare efficiency with and without punishment, it provides only indirect support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Richter, A., & Grasman, J. (2013). The transmission of sustainable harvesting norms when agents are conditionally cooperative. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the presence of conditional cooperators in a common-pool resource setting can lead to complex and sometimes abrupt transitions between cooperative and non-cooperative equilibria. Efficiency, measured as resource stock and group profit relative to the maximum economic yield, is highly sensitive to social dynamics. When temptation to defect increases or when group size increases, the system can shift from high-efficiency (cooperative) to low-efficiency (defective) states, sometimes with hysteresis or limit cycles. The model highlights that social norm enforcement via moral persuasion can sustain cooperation, but is vulnerable to collapse if conditional cooperators lose motivation. The absence of explicit punishment means that norm enforcement is 'soft' and collapses are possible even without changes in external parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like common-pool resource games, the efficiency of group outcomes is highly sensitive to the social composition (intrinsic vs. conditional cooperators vs. defectors) and to the strength of norm enforcement mechanisms. While it does not model explicit punishment, it suggests that in the absence of strong enforcement (e.g., costly punishment), efficiency is fragile and can collapse due to endogenous social dynamics. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that enabling or strengthening norm enforcement (potentially via punishment) could stabilize high-efficiency outcomes, especially in larger groups or when temptation to defect is high. However, the model also warns that even with some enforcement, efficiency can be lost if social dynamics shift. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is absent or weak, and thus provide a baseline for understanding the potential value of adding punishment.

- source: Richter, A., van Soest, D., & Grasman, J. (2013). Contagious cooperation, temptation, and ecosystem collapse. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a closed community harvesting a renewable resource, cooperation can be sustained through social norm contagion, but is vulnerable to collapse if the temptation to defect becomes too strong (e.g., due to increased population or technology). The system can flip from a high-efficiency (cooperative) equilibrium to a low-efficiency (defective) equilibrium, with substantial welfare loss. Hysteresis is present: reversing the parameter change that caused collapse is not sufficient to restore cooperation. The model provides explicit formulas for welfare under cooperation and Nash equilibrium, showing large efficiency losses after collapse. The presence of social norm diffusion (but not explicit punishment) can sustain cooperation up to a tipping point.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding the efficiency dynamics of common-pool resource games with social norm diffusion, but it does not model explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where cooperation is maintained by social pressure, persuasion, or contagion, rather than by costly punishment. The model shows that efficiency can be high and robust up to a tipping point, after which collapse is sudden and efficiency is lost. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence about the role of population size, technology, and social norm strength in determining efficiency, but does not provide direct evidence about the effect of enabling or disabling punishment. It is best used as a theoretical baseline for environments without explicit punishment.

- source: Hashim, M. J., Kannan, K. N., Maximiano, S., & Ulmer, J. R. (2014). Digital Piracy, Teens, and the Source of Advice: An Experimental Study. *Journal of Management Information Systems*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Advice from parents, especially when they are punished for their child's piracy, increases purchasing and group profit and reduces piracy compared to other advice sources. Advice from record labels is the least effective and can even reduce group profit. Advice from a regulator is largely ignored. The effect of advice is strongest when the adviser has both a social tie and a stake in the outcome. The study does not include standard peer punishment or reward mechanisms; the only 'punishment' is an exogenous penalty to parents in one treatment. The game is a close variant of a public goods game, but not a standard PGG. Efficiency-related outcomes (group profit, purchasing) are reported, but not as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that exogenous punishment of a third party (parents) for a player's free-riding can increase public good provision and group profit, especially when combined with a social tie. However, it does not test endogenous peer punishment or standard PGG punishment institutions. The findings suggest that punishment mechanisms may be more effective when they are socially salient and affect someone with a close tie to the player, but direct transfer to standard PGG with peer punishment is limited. Control efficiency (group profit without punishment) is reported, and the effect of introducing a punishment-like mechanism (parent penalty) can be inferred, but the mechanism differs from standard punishment. The absence of peer punishment, reward, or communication means the results are only adjacent for direct prediction of PGG punishment effects.

- source: Dickson, E. S., Gordon, S. C., & Huber, G. A. (2015). Institutional Sources of Legitimate Authority: An Experimental Investigation. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that citizens are more willing to help salaried (fixed-compensation) authorities than those who personally benefit from collected penalties, but only when transparency is high. When transparency is low, support for salaried authorities collapses, while support for appropriator authorities is low regardless of transparency. Contribution rates and average earnings are reported, but do not differ significantly between treatments under full information; under limited information, contribution rates are lower for salaried authorities. The main outcome is a behavioral measure of legitimacy (citizen intervention), not efficiency or group payoff. The deterrence index (successful punishment of noncontributors minus punishment of contributors) is associated with higher contributions, but the causal effect of punishment on efficiency is not directly estimated.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how institutional features (transparency, authority incentives) affect citizen support for enforcement and, through deterrence, contribution rates. However, it does not directly estimate the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency or payoff. The design is a binary (all-or-nothing) public goods game with centralized, probabilistic punishment and a unique intervention stage. The findings suggest that the legitimacy of punishment (as measured by citizen support) is sensitive to transparency and authority incentives, which may in turn affect deterrence and contributions, but the direct impact on efficiency is not quantified. For prediction tasks, this paper is informative about mechanisms and moderators but does not provide direct treatment effect estimates for efficiency.

- source: McIntosh, C., Sadoulet, E., Buck, S., & Rosada, T. (2013). Reputation in a public goods game: Taking the design of credit bureaus to the lab. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that both group-level and individual-level reputation mechanisms (used for reassigning ejected players) increase average contributions in a repeated public goods game with ejection, compared to a random reassignment baseline. The effect size is about a 7.5% increase in average contribution (from ~75% to ~82% of the endowment). There is no significant difference in average contribution between group and individual information treatments. Ejection is more sensitive to individual contribution in the group information treatment. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the contribution data can be mapped to efficiency. The ejection mechanism is not a standard punishment technology (no direct costly reduction of others' payoffs), but rather exclusion from play. The findings suggest that group-based reputation is at least as effective as individual-based reputation in sustaining cooperation, and that transitioning microfinance credit bureaus from group to individual reporting may not yield substantial gains.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with ejection (exclusion) as the sanction mechanism, both group-level and individual-level reputation mechanisms increase average contributions compared to random reassignment. The effect is similar for both information types. While the study does not report efficiency directly, the increase in average contribution implies a corresponding increase in efficiency. The ejection mechanism is not a standard punishment technology (no direct costly punishment), so the results are most relevant for environments where exclusion or ostracism is the main sanction. The findings suggest that, for games with endogenous group selection and ejection, introducing a reputation-based reassignment mechanism (either group or individual) can improve cooperation and implied efficiency, but the marginal benefit of individual-level over group-level information is minimal. The results are less directly informative for standard PGGs with costly punishment, but may be useful for predicting the effect of exclusionary sanctions.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2016). How the expanded crowd-funding mechanism of some southern rural areas in China affects cooperative behaviors in threshold public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that both publishing the list of cooperators and the presence of lobbyists can significantly increase the number of cooperators in a threshold public goods game, especially when the synergy factor is not too high. The effectiveness of these mechanisms depends on the population structure (proportion of unconditional cooperators vs. defectors), the synergy factor, and emotional influence parameters. When the synergy factor is high, publishing the list is always beneficial and the role of lobbyists becomes negligible. When the synergy factor is low, the decision to publish the list should consider the relative numbers of cooperators and defectors and the strength of emotional inhibition. The paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how social recognition (publishing the list) and persuasive agents (lobbyists) can increase cooperation rates in threshold public goods games, especially under certain parameter regimes. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment or similar mechanisms on efficiency. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral pathways by which social mechanisms affect cooperation, but should be used with caution for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Du, J. M., Wu, B., & Wang, L. (2014). Climate collective risk dilemma with feedback of real-time temperatures. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The model demonstrates that coupling real-time environmental feedback (temperature) to payoffs in a threshold public goods game can promote and stabilize cooperation. When the public good threshold is not met, temperature rises, which reduces all players' payoffs (environmental punishment). This feedback leads to cyclical dynamics where cooperation rebounds as payoffs fall, stabilizing temperature near a reference level. The model outperforms versions without feedback in both temperature control and cooperation rates. However, the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, and the 'punishment' is environmental rather than peer-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only indirectly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The 'punishment' mechanism is environmental (temperature rise reducing payoffs for all), not a peer or institutional punishment option. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but shows that environmental feedback can stabilize cooperation and temperature. The findings suggest that automatic, group-level sanctions (environmental feedback) can promote cooperation, but the results may not transfer directly to settings with explicit punishment options. Use this paper for contextual or mechanistic insight, not for direct quantitative prediction.

- source: Hurlstone, M. J., Wang, S., Price, A., Leviston, Z., & Walker, I. (2017). Cooperation studies of catastrophe avoidance: implications for climate negotiations. *Climatic Change*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in collective-risk social dilemma games is strongly affected by perceived risk, inequality, uncertainty about thresholds, intergenerational discounting, and the possibility of reputation-based rewards or punishments. High perceived risk, communication (pledges), intermediate targets, and reputation mechanisms can increase cooperation (i.e., the proportion of groups that avert catastrophe). Inequality and uncertainty about the threshold for catastrophe reduce cooperation. The paper discusses the need for credible enforcement mechanisms (punishment) in real-world climate agreements but does not review experiments that directly implement or vary punishment in the game. The main findings are about behavioral cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence about the importance of enforcement (punishment) mechanisms for sustaining cooperation in public-goods-like threshold games, but does not provide direct experimental evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. It is useful for understanding moderators of cooperation and the conceptual role of punishment, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in a public goods game. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that credible punishment mechanisms are likely to be important, especially under uncertainty, but does not provide data or effect sizes.

- source: Aktipis, A. (2016). Principles of cooperation across systems: from human sharing to multicellularity and cancer. *Evolutionary Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper identifies three general principles that facilitate cooperation: (1) the ability to 'Walk Away' from uncooperative partners, (2) need-based resource transfers, and (3) the necessity of increasingly sophisticated cheater suppression mechanisms as cooperation scales up. It reviews agent-based models (from prior work) showing that conditional movement (Walk Away) can stabilize cooperation in both dyadic and group contexts, including public goods game-like environments. The paper discusses how positive assortment (cooperators interacting more with each other) is key to the evolution of cooperation. It also highlights the challenges of cheater suppression in large-scale systems and draws analogies between human societies, multicellular organisms, and social insects. However, it does not provide new empirical or experimental results on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it directly analyze the effects of punishment mechanisms in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical background on mechanisms that support cooperation, such as partner choice (Walk Away) and cheater suppression, which are relevant to understanding the effects of punishment in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct empirical or quantitative evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. The discussion of cheater suppression includes punishment as one possible mechanism but is not specific or detailed enough to inform quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The most relevant insight is that as cooperative systems scale up, more sophisticated and costly cheater suppression mechanisms (including punishment) are required, but the paper does not specify the efficiency consequences of these mechanisms in controlled game environments.

- source: Centola, D. (2013). A Simple Model of Stability in Critical Mass Dynamics. *Journal of Statistical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that weakly self-reinforcing collective action systems (where incentives to participate peak before full participation, allowing for free riders) are more robust to perturbations and have greater long-term stability than strongly self-reinforcing systems (where incentives increase monotonically with participation and free riding is eliminated). Strongly self-reinforcing systems can achieve higher participation but are more vulnerable to collapse from stochastic shocks. The presence of free riders in weakly self-reinforcing systems provides a reserve of potential contributors that stabilizes participation when it drops. The analysis is theoretical and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into the trade-off between maximizing participation (and thus, potentially, efficiency) and ensuring long-term stability in collective action systems. While it does not directly address efficiency or payoff outcomes, it suggests that systems designed to eliminate free riding (e.g., via strong incentives or possibly punishment) may be less stable, and thus may not always achieve higher long-term group benefit. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that the relationship between punishment (or strong incentives) and efficiency may be moderated by stability concerns, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for efficiency effects. The model is most relevant for understanding the dynamics of participation and the potential unintended consequences of interventions aimed at increasing cooperation.

- source: Bernard, M., Dreber, A., Strimling, P., & Eriksson, K. (2013). The subgroup problem: When can binding voting on extractions from a common pool resource overcome the tragedy of the commons?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment compares four treatments in a repeated static common pool resource (CPR) game: (1) Autonomy (individual extraction), (2) Global Democracy (all vote, binding for all), (3) Local Democracy (subgroup voting, binding within subgroup), and (4) Delegation (random subgroup leader votes, binding for all). Efficiency, measured by mean profit per round and mean extraction, is highest in Global Democracy and Delegation (close to social optimum), lowest in Autonomy (close to Nash equilibrium), and intermediate in Local Democracy (closer to Nash than to optimum, and deteriorating over time). The results show that only globally binding voting (direct or via delegation) reliably overcomes the tragedy of the commons, while local/subgroup voting is insufficient. No explicit punishment or sanctioning is present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in CPR games with the design features described, introducing a binding group-level voting mechanism (even via randomly selected leaders) can dramatically increase efficiency compared to individual decision-making. However, binding voting limited to subgroups is less effective and does not achieve full efficiency. The absence of explicit punishment or sanctioning means the results are not directly transferable to settings where punishment is the treatment of interest, but the findings are highly informative for predicting the effect of institutional design (voting scope) on efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that group-level binding rules (even without punishment) can substitute for or complement punishment in achieving high efficiency, while subgroup-level rules are less effective.

- source: Majolo, B., & Maréchal, L. (2017). Between-group competition elicits within-group cooperation in children. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that simulated between-group competition (BGC) increases within-group cooperation among children aged 3-10, as measured by the number of stickers shared with group members (experiment 1) and the number of stickers contributed to a public good (experiment 2). The effect is robust to age (especially for children 6+), and is not explained by gender, kinship, or familiarity. However, the experiments do not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and do not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The main dependent variables are behavioral measures of cooperation, not payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does provide indirect evidence that between-group competition can increase cooperative behavior in children, but since punishment is not manipulated or measured, and efficiency or group payoff is not reported, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, MPCR) are partially reported, but the absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes means this paper should not be weighted heavily for the prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Diekmann, A., & Przepiorka, W. (2016). Take One for the Team! Individual Heterogeneity and the Emergence of Latent Norms in a Volunteer's Dilemma. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in repeated volunteer's dilemma games, efficient public good provision emerges through different behavioral patterns depending on group structure. In symmetric groups with fixed partners, turn-taking emerges, leading to high efficiency and equal payoffs. In asymmetric groups, especially when the lowest-cost actor is much stronger or when roles are randomized each round, solitary volunteering by the strong actor dominates, also leading to high efficiency. When distributional conflict is present (fixed roles), efficiency is somewhat lower due to reluctance of strong actors to always volunteer. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and thus provides no evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any condition. However, it is informative about baseline efficiency levels in repeated volunteer's dilemma games under various structural conditions (symmetry/asymmetry, matching protocol). For prediction tasks, this paper can inform the expected efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions for step-level public good games with similar parameters, but cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment. It is not directly useful for predicting treatment efficiency when punishment is enabled.

- source: Skatova, A., Spence, A., Leygue, C., & Ferguson, E. (2017). Guilty repair sustains cooperation, angry retaliation destroys it. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated social dilemma game without punishment, group efficiency (measured as group earnings and energy savings) declines over time due to increased defection and retaliation. Retaliation (increasing usage after being exploited) is associated with lower group payoffs, while repair (reducing usage after overusing) is associated with higher group payoffs. Groups with more prosocial members (who are more likely to repair and less likely to retaliate) achieve higher efficiency. Emotional responses (anger and guilt) mediate these behaviors: anger leads to retaliation, guilt leads to repair. The absence of punishment means that emotional self-regulation, especially guilt-driven repair, is the main mechanism for sustaining cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in repeated public-goods-like games without punishment, efficiency tends to decline over time due to retaliation and lack of repair. The presence of prosocial individuals who engage in guilty repair can partially sustain efficiency, but overall, the lack of punishment leads to lower group payoffs. For prediction tasks, this study suggests that in the absence of punishment, efficiency will be lower and decline over time, especially in groups with more selfish individuals. The findings are most relevant for control conditions (no punishment), and highlight the importance of emotional and social preference heterogeneity. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment, so it cannot inform the incremental effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Efferson, C., Roca, C. P., Vogt, S., & Helbing, D. (2016). Sustained cooperation by running away from bad behavior. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Allowing players to move away from their current neighborhood (endogenous movement) in a spatial social dilemma leads to higher cooperation rates and, crucially, eliminates the average payoff disadvantage of cooperation compared to defection. This is achieved through positive assortment: cooperators cluster together, shielding themselves from defectors. In the control condition (assign location), where movement is exogenous, cooperation remains costly and less frequent. The experiment does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so the results are about migration/partner choice as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. Instead, it demonstrates that allowing migration/partner choice can eliminate the payoff disadvantage of cooperation, even without punishment. For prediction tasks about punishment effects, this paper is not directly informative, but it does highlight that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., migration) can also support efficiency. If a game design includes migration but not punishment, this paper is highly relevant; if the focus is on punishment, it is not.

- source: Feltovich, N., & Grossman, P. J. (2015). How does the effect of pre-play suggestions vary with group size? Experimental evidence from a threshold public-good game. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that pre-play cheap-talk communication (suggestions) significantly increases cooperation, the frequency of efficient (all-cooperate) outcomes, and group payoffs in a one-shot threshold public-good game, but only for small groups. As group size increases, the positive effect of communication on efficiency and payoffs declines rapidly, becoming statistically insignificant or negative for groups of size 7 and 15. This pattern holds for both one-way (random leader) and all-way (full broadcast) communication treatments. The results are robust to controls for risk preferences and other covariates. The study highlights the importance of group size as a moderator of communication's effectiveness in promoting efficient outcomes in public-good-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of communication (pre-play suggestions) on efficiency in threshold public-good games is highly dependent on group size. For small groups (n=2,3), enabling communication can substantially increase efficiency relative to control. For larger groups (n=7,15), the effect is minimal or even negative. When predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, group size should be treated as a key moderator of communication's impact. The absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms means these results are most applicable to settings where only communication is varied. The findings suggest that extrapolating positive communication effects from small-group experiments to larger groups is not warranted.

- source: Brekke, K. A., Konow, J., & Nyborg, K. (2017). Framing in a threshold public goods experiment with heterogeneous endowments. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The experiment finds that high-endowed subjects contribute more in absolute terms than low-endowed subjects, but both groups contribute similar proportions of their endowments except when the contribution metric is framed in absolute terms, in which case low-endowed subjects contribute significantly more (about 80% of their endowment). The vast majority of groups reach the threshold for public good provision, often in the first period. Framing effects are present for low-endowed subjects but not for high-endowed subjects. Distributive preferences explain some of the variation in contributions, especially for high-endowed subjects, but do not explain the framing effect for low-endowed subjects. No punishment or sanctioning is present or studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. However, it does provide informative context on how framing and endowment heterogeneity affect contribution behavior and project success rates in threshold public good games with communication. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding baseline contribution behavior and the role of framing in similar environments without punishment.

- source: Chung, D., Yun, K., & Jeong, J. (2015). Decoding covert motivations of free riding and cooperation from multi-feature pattern analysis of EEG signals. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that neural oscillations measured by EEG immediately after result presentation can predict whether a participant will cooperate or free ride in the next round, across three different payoff structures that manipulate motivations (fear and greed). Behaviorally, free riding rates are highest in the standard condition, lower in the 'no fear' condition, and lowest in the 'no greed' condition. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. It may provide some indirect context about how payoff structure (fear/greed) affects cooperation rates, but is not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Boosey, L. A. (2017). Conditional cooperation in network public goods experiments. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a network public goods game, average contributions decline over time, with the rate and extent of decay influenced by the type of information feedback and the composition of the group. Feedback about payoffs (rather than contributions) leads to lower contributions. The presence of a free-rider accelerates the decay of contributions, especially for players closer to the free-rider in the network. Unconditional full contributors ('good eggs') can slow the decline in contributions for their immediate neighbors, but do not induce convergence to full cooperation. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms and does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it is informative about the baseline dynamics of contribution decay and the influence of information feedback and group composition in networked PGGs. For prediction tasks, this paper can help establish expectations for control (no-punishment) efficiency in networked environments, especially regarding the impact of feedback format and the presence of free-riders or full contributors. It does not inform the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2014). Excessive abundance of common resources deters social responsibility. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in a collective-risk social dilemma is most sustainable when the initial amount of common resources and the effectiveness of cooperative contributions are at intermediate levels. Excessive abundance of resources or overly effective cooperation allows defectors to free-ride, leading to the eventual collapse of cooperation and resource depletion. Conversely, too little resource or ineffective cooperation also leads to collapse. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The main reported outcomes are the stationary fraction of cooperators and the sustainability of the common resource pool, not direct efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as defined in the prediction task. Its findings about resource abundance and cooperation may be contextually informative for understanding resource dynamics, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Xiao, E., & Kunreuther, H. (2016). Punishment and Cooperation in Stochastic Social Dilemmas. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In two-person repeated prisoner's dilemma games, both deterministic and stochastic (with outcome uncertainty), the restricted punishment mechanism (only cooperators can punish defectors) increases cooperation rates compared to no-punishment and unrestricted punishment. However, the efficiency gains (measured by average earnings) from restricted punishment are significant only in deterministic environments; in stochastic environments, restricted punishment does not significantly increase average earnings compared to no-punishment. Unrestricted punishment leads to frequent retaliation and antisocial punishment, resulting in lower average earnings than the baseline in both environments. The effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation and efficiency is reduced by outcome uncertainty, as noncooperators are less likely to be punished when outcomes are stochastic.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends on both the structure of the punishment mechanism (restricted vs unrestricted) and the presence of outcome uncertainty (stochastic vs deterministic). In games with two players, repeated for 10 rounds, with perfect information and no communication, restricted punishment can increase efficiency in deterministic settings but not in stochastic ones. Unrestricted punishment can reduce efficiency due to retaliation and antisocial punishment. For prediction, the presence of outcome uncertainty and the type of punishment mechanism are key moderators: restricted punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when outcomes are deterministic and less so when outcomes are stochastic. Control efficiency (no-punishment) is similar in both environments, but the efficiency boost from punishment is context-dependent.

- source: Roux, C., & Thöni, C. (2015). Collusion among many firms: The disciplinary power of targeted punishment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The availability of targeted punishment in Cournot oligopoly experiments leads to significantly higher group profits and lower total output (closer to the collusive benchmark) for all group sizes tested (2, 4, 6, 8). The effect of punishment on efficiency is positive and increases with group size beyond duopoly: in 6- and 8-firm markets, mean profits nearly double with punishment. Punishment is used in a targeted and proportional way, with defectors facing harsher penalties as group size increases. The study provides direct evidence that targeted punishment can sustain high-efficiency collusion even in large groups, contrary to standard theory and previous experimental results without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence that enabling targeted punishment in repeated Cournot (oligopoly) games leads to large increases in group efficiency (profit), especially as group size increases. The design is not a standard public goods game but is closely related in structure and in the punishment mechanism. The results suggest that, for games with similar punishment technology (high impact, targeted, known cost), the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be much higher than in the control (no-punishment) condition, and the effect is larger for larger groups. The findings are highly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions, especially for settings with targeted, high-impact punishment and no communication.

- source: Feng, T. Y., Tai, S. L., Sun, C. S., & Man, Q. P. (2017). Study on Cooperative Mechanism of Prefabricated Producers Based on Evolutionary Game Theory. *Mathematical Problems in Engineering*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper models cooperation between two prefabricated producers using evolutionary game theory. It finds that, in the absence of external mechanisms, both mutual cooperation and mutual defection are stable equilibria, and the outcome depends on initial conditions. Introducing a punishment mechanism (where defectors are fined and the fine is transferred to the cooperator) or a sufficiently large incentive (reward) for cooperation changes the game so that only mutual cooperation is stable, provided the punishment or reward exceeds a threshold related to the cost of cooperation. This leads to a win-win outcome and maximizes group efficiency. The analysis is theoretical and does not provide empirical data, but it directly addresses the effect of punishment on group payoff and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in a two-player repeated game with payoff structure similar to a stag hunt or coordination game, introducing a sufficiently strong punishment mechanism (where defectors are fined and the fine is transferred to the cooperator) ensures that mutual cooperation is the only stable outcome, maximizing group efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar settings, enabling punishment (with sufficient strength) will reliably increase efficiency from potentially low (if initial conditions favor defection) to maximal (full cooperation). However, the model is limited to two players, does not include stochasticity or empirical heterogeneity, and assumes rational updating. The findings are most applicable to small, symmetric, repeated games with clear punishment transfer.

- source: Moser, S., & Musshoff, O. (2016). Ex-ante Evaluation of Policy Measures: Effects of Reward and Punishment for Fertiliser Reduction in Palm Oil Production. *Journal of Agricultural Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that, holding expected income effects constant, the design of incentives (reward vs. punishment, certain vs. uncertain) significantly affects both behavioral response and efficiency. Certain rewards are the most efficient (i.e., minimize profit loss for a given reduction in fertiliser use), while uncertain punishments are the least efficient. Certain punishments and uncertain rewards are intermediate. Punishment is less efficient than reward, and uncertainty reduces efficiency. The findings suggest that policymakers aiming for efficient reduction of negative externalities should prefer certain rewards over punishments or uncertain incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a public-good-like field context, the efficiency impact of punishment (vs. reward) depends strongly on the design: punishment is less efficient than reward, and uncertainty in the incentive (probabilistic punishment or reward) further reduces efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment (especially uncertain punishment) will not increase efficiency as much as enabling certain rewards, even if the expected value is held constant. The results are most applicable to field settings with individual decision-making and externalities, but less so to classic multi-player PGGs with peer punishment. The efficiency outcome is measured as profit difference, directly supporting payoff-based prediction.

- source: Grimalda, G., Pondorfer, A., & Tracer, D. P. (2016). Social image concerns promote cooperation more than altruistic punishment. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a one-shot, two-player prisoner's dilemma, the presence of a local authority (Big Man) as an observer increases cooperation and leads to the highest average payoffs, exceeding baseline. Introducing a costly punishment stage (where players can spend to reduce the other's payoff) does not increase cooperation above the social image condition and results in lower average payoffs than baseline, due to the costs incurred by both punisher and punished. When both social image and punishment are present, efficiency is still lower than with social image alone and remains below baseline. The presence of an external (outgroup) authority does not increase cooperation or payoffs. Thus, punishment is not an efficient mechanism for promoting cooperation in this setting, and social image concerns are more effective.
