The structure of the interaction network (degree, heterogeneity) modulates these effects: higher degree or more heterogeneous networks promote bluffing and can make punishment less effective. System bias (delta) that increases conflict frequency also enhances the effectiveness of punishment. Players with higher real ability tend to evolve higher overconfidence and bluffing. The paper provides some analysis of average payoff for punished vs. non-punished players, showing that those with lower ability and lower payoff are punished more often, but does not report group-level efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and probability of punishment can reduce the prevalence of overconfidence and bluffing in resource competition games, especially overconfidence. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (deception levels) and not group efficiency or total payoff, the direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes in public goods or similar games is limited. The model is adjacent to PGGs: it is a resource competition game with pairwise contests, not a standard PGG. The punishment mechanism is modeled as a reduction in deception traits rather than direct payoff reduction. The paper does provide some subgroup payoff analysis, suggesting that punishment targets and reduces the payoff of lower-ability, higher-deception individuals, but does not quantify overall group efficiency. Thus, the main decision support is qualitative: punishment reduces harmful behavioral biases, and its effectiveness depends on network structure and conflict frequency, but the effect on group efficiency is not directly established.

- source: Boehm, C. (2014). The moral consequences of social selection. *Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in mobile hunter-gatherer societies, group punishment—ranging from shaming and ostracism to capital punishment—has been a central mechanism for enforcing cooperation and curbing antisocial behaviors such as bullying, theft, and cheating. These sanctions are argued to have shaped the evolution of human conscience and moral norms, favoring individuals who are generous, honest, and humble. The author suggests that such punitive social selection has been critical for the emergence of egalitarian, cooperative societies, and for the evolutionary success of altruistic traits. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative analysis of efficiency or group payoffs in public goods game-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and ethnographic context for the role of punishment in promoting cooperation and suppressing free-riding in small-scale societies. It supports the idea that punishment can be a powerful tool for maintaining cooperation, but does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper offers indirect support for the positive role of punishment in sustaining cooperation, especially in small, face-to-face groups, but does not allow for parameter-specific predictions based on game design dimensions.

- source: Du, E., & Chang, S. W. C. (2015). Neural components of altruistic punishment. *Frontiers in Neuroscience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper reviews evidence that altruistic punishment is driven by neural processes related to inequity aversion, cost-benefit analysis, and social reference framing. It highlights the involvement of brain regions such as the insula, prefrontal cortex, and anterior cingulate cortex in these processes. The review draws on studies using ultimatum, dictator, and trust games, but does not focus on public goods games or report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the psychological and neural basis of punishment decisions, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods game-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is useful for understanding the cognitive and neural mechanisms that may underlie punishment behavior, which could inform mechanistic models, but it does not report or synthesize payoff-based outcomes or moderators relevant to efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper offers only indirect, mechanistic context and does not inform quantitative or qualitative predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Singh, M., & Boomsma, J. J. (2015). Policing and punishment across the domains of social evolution. *Oikos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper proposes that punishment and policing behaviors are maintained when the inclusive fitness benefits to the punisher outweigh the costs, and when these benefits are greater than those from bystanding. The framework is applied to a range of biological and social systems, showing that power, relatedness, and reproductive options drive variation in who punishes. In systems with strong power asymmetries, dominants are more likely to punish; in systems with high relatedness and low individual reproductive prospects, policing is more likely to be corrective and group-beneficial. The paper highlights that the same inclusive fitness logic can explain punishment across diverse domains, but does not provide empirical data or direct predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a conceptual framework for understanding the conditions under which punishment is likely to emerge and be maintained in social groups, emphasizing the roles of power, relatedness, and reproductive options. However, it does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. For the downstream prediction task, the paper may inform mechanistic expectations about when punishment is likely to be effective or who will implement it, but it does not supply data or direct evidence to calibrate efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions.

- source: Lopez, A. C. (2017). The Evolutionary Psychology of War: Offense and Defense in the Adapted Mind. *Evolutionary Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that (1) willingness to participate in collective aggression is predicted by expected group benefit in defense (public good) and by expected personal benefit in offense (private/club good); (2) punitive sentiment toward nonparticipants is more strongly triggered by willingness to participate in defense than in offense, supporting the idea that punishment is used to enforce participation in public goods contexts; (3) reward sentiment is also modulated by conflict type, with willingness to participate predicting reward sentiment in offense, and expected benefit predicting it in defense; (4) sex differences exist, with males generally more willing to participate, but labor sufficiency cues can increase female participation in defense; (5) labor sufficiency/insufficiency manipulations affect both willingness to participate and punitive sentiment, especially in defense. However, all outcomes are self-reported attitudes or intentions in hypothetical scenarios, not actual behavior or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, conceptual support for the idea that punishment is more likely to be used and to have a motivational effect in public goods (defense) contexts than in private/club goods (offense) contexts, and that the effectiveness of punishment as a recruitment/enforcement tool depends on the structure of the collective action problem and cues about labor sufficiency. However, it does not provide any direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency, group payoff, or realized cooperation in public goods games or similar environments. The findings may inform predictions about when punishment will be more or less effective as a cooperation-enforcing mechanism, especially in relation to the publicness of the good and the perceived need for labor, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes. The study is best used for mechanism and moderator hypotheses, not for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Li, Y., Fermin, A. S. R., Kanai, R., Takagishi, H., Matsumoto, Y., Kiyonari, T., & Sakagami, M. (2017). Behavioural Differences and Neural Substrates of Altruistic and Spiteful Punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that individuals who engage in costly punishment in the TPPG (altruistic punishers) also behave more pro-socially in a variety of non-strategic games, while those who only reject unfair offers in the UG (spiteful punishers) behave more selfishly. The distinction is supported by neural data: spiteful punishers have larger left caudate nucleus and nucleus accumbens volumes. The paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff, nor does it manipulate the presence or absence of punishment in a public goods or social dilemma context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or social dilemma games. It does, however, suggest that the motivations behind punishment (altruistic vs. spiteful) are heterogeneous and that not all punishment is associated with pro-social or efficiency-enhancing behavior. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment may not always increase efficiency, depending on the underlying motives of punishers, but it does not provide quantitative or design-specific evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Hilbig, B. E., Thielmann, I., Klein, S. A., & Henninger, F. (2016). The two faces of cooperation: On the unique role of HEXACO Agreeableness for forgiveness versus retaliation. *Journal of Research in Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in the Uncostly Retaliation Game, participants are much more likely to retaliate (punish) after being treated unfairly, and that this retaliation is predicted by lower Agreeableness (HEXACO AG), but not by Honesty-Humility (HH). Retaliation after fair treatment (antisocial punishment) is rare and is negatively predicted by Honesty-Humility. The game structure ensures that punishment is uncostly to the punisher, isolating the psychological motive for retaliation. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported. The results are robust across two experiments and are not explained by social value orientation or inequality aversion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar multi-player, repeated games. Its main contribution is to show that the psychological trait of Agreeableness predicts individual differences in uncostly punishment (retaliation) after unfair treatment in a two-player, one-shot allocation game. The game design is adjacent to public goods games in that it involves punishment for norm violations, but it lacks the group, repeated, and efficiency elements central to the prediction task. The findings may inform models of individual punishment behavior, but not efficiency outcomes.

- source: Kurz, T., Thomas, W. E., & Fonseca, M. A. (2014). A fine is a more effective financial deterrent when framed retributively and extracted publicly. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The field experiment demonstrates that the effectiveness of financial punishment in deterring undesired behavior (lateness) depends strongly on both the framing (retributive vs. compensatory) and the public/private nature of the sanction. Retributive and public fines significantly increase punctuality compared to compensatory or private fines, which are no more effective than no fine. A follow-up scenario study shows that compensatory fines reduce the perceived immorality of transgression, while retributive fines do not. The results support a theory that the moral framing and public visibility of punishment are key moderators of its behavioral impact.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the framing and public administration of punishment can substantially moderate its behavioral effectiveness, even when the material cost is held constant. While the context is not a public goods game, the findings suggest that in collective action settings, punishment will be more effective at promoting cooperation if it is framed as retributive (moral) and administered publicly. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so direct prediction of efficiency effects is not possible. The results are most useful for informing the design of punishment mechanisms (e.g., public vs. private, moral vs. compensatory framing) rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Berger, J. (2016). The Sanctioning Dilemma: A Quasi-Experiment on Social Norm Enforcement in the Train. *European Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that peer-sanctioning (negative sanctioning of a norm violator) occurs in about 50% of interventions. Contrary to the classic diffusion of responsibility hypothesis, the likelihood of sanctioning increases with the number of passengers present. Passengers who are closer to the norm breaker and those whose activities require silence are more likely to sanction. The likelihood of sanctioning decreases as the train approaches its destination. No significant difference in sanctioning rates is found between silent-area and non-silent-area train cars. The study does not measure or report any payoff, efficiency, or group welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their variants. While it is conceptually adjacent—framing norm enforcement as a second-order public good and using volunteer's dilemma theory—the outcomes are strictly behavioral (sanctioning occurrence and timing). There is no measurement of group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, nor any manipulation of punishment cost, effectiveness, or related game design dimensions. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments, though it may provide some indirect, conceptual support for the importance of heterogeneity and group size in norm enforcement contexts.

- source: Berger, J., & Hevenstone, D. (2016). Norm enforcement in the city revisited: An international field experiment of altruistic punishment, norm maintenance, and broken windows. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that direct and indirect norm enforcement behaviors (including costly punishment) occur in natural, anonymous, one-shot interactions, but at lower rates than in laboratory experiments. Enforcement is more common in Switzerland than in New York City. The presence of disorder (litter) reduces the likelihood of both direct and indirect enforcement. Making it easier to maintain the norm (by being close to a trashcan) increases norm maintenance behaviors. Subtle sanctions and norm maintenance are more frequent than direct confrontation. Individual characteristics (in-group status, age) also predict enforcement. The study does not report any efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, so its findings are not directly transferable to predictions about group efficiency in public goods games with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that costly punishment and other forms of norm enforcement are less frequent in real-world, anonymous, one-shot settings than in laboratory public goods games. It also shows that environmental context (disorder, opportunity structure) and location matter for the frequency of enforcement. However, because the study does not measure group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value for prediction is as indirect, contextual evidence about the real-world prevalence and moderators of costly punishment, suggesting that laboratory findings may overestimate the frequency and possibly the efficiency impact of punishment in naturalistic settings.

- source: Mathew, S. (2017). How the Second-Order Free Rider Problem Is Solved in a Small-Scale Society. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that Turkana adults express strong disapproval toward second-order free riders (those who do not punish norm violators), as well as toward those who punish excessively, unjustly, or without proper group consensus. Punishment is seen as legitimate only when collectively decided and administered by a preordained group. These meta-norms help regulate peer punishment and may solve the second-order free rider problem in this society. However, the evidence is based on responses to hypothetical scenarios, not on observed behavior or economic outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that strong social norms and meta-norms can support the legitimacy and regulation of peer punishment in a real-world society, potentially solving the second-order free rider problem. However, because the study does not measure efficiency, group payoff, or actual economic outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of meta-norms and collective legitimacy for the functioning of peer punishment systems.

- source: Eriksson, K., Andersson, P. A., & Strimling, P. (2016). Moderators of the disapproval of peer punishment. *Group Processes & Intergroup Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that peer punishment of norm violators is typically disapproved of by observers, especially when the punishment is severe or appears aggressive. This disapproval extends to viewing the punisher as a problem for the group and the act as inappropriate. Disapproval is moderated by the observer's own trait aggression (less disapproval among more aggressive raters), the severity of punishment (more severe = more disapproval), and whether the punishment is collective or individual (collective = slightly less disapproval). Economic sanctions are disapproved of less than physical ones, and unselfish economic sanctions (where the punisher does not profit) are disapproved of less than selfish ones. Verbal sanctions are also disapproved of, even when effective. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any related outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding social approval and the perceived legitimacy of peer punishment, but not for predicting how punishment affects group efficiency or earnings. The design is adjacent to PGGs (common resource, norm violation, sanctioning), but the outcomes are purely attitudinal and perceptual. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task of treatment efficiency, though it may inform hypotheses about the social acceptability of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Eriksson, K., Strimling, P., Andersson, P. A., Aveyard, M., Brauer, M., Gritskov, V., Kiyonari, T., Kuhlman, D. M., Maitner, A. T., Manesi, Z., Molho, C., Peperkoorn, L. S., Rizwan, M., Stivers, A. W., Tian, Q. R., Van Lange, P. A. M., Vartanova, I., Wu, J. H., & Yamagishi, T. (2017). Cultural Universals and Cultural Differences in Meta-Norms about Peer Punishment. *Management and Organization Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that across eight countries, punishment of norm violators in a resource-sharing scenario is judged as more appropriate when enacted by the group rather than by an individual. There is substantial cross-cultural variation in the appraisal of punishers versus non-punishers: in more individualistic countries (USA, Netherlands, Sweden, Japan), punishers are rated less favorably than non-punishers, while in more collectivistic countries (Pakistan, UAE, Russia, China), this difference is absent or reversed. These patterns hold for both retributive and restorative forms of punishment. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any related payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are limited to social appraisals and meta-norms about punishment, not behavioral or payoff outcomes. However, it may inform predictions about the acceptability or likelihood of punishment being used in different cultural contexts, which could indirectly affect the implementation or effectiveness of punishment in real-world or experimental settings. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as providing only contextual or indirect evidence, not direct evidence about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment.

- source: Gordon, D. S., & Lea, S. E. G. (2016). Who Punishes? The Status of the Punishers Affects the Perceived Success of, and Indirect Benefits From, Moralistic'' Punishment. *Evolutionary Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_punishment_id
  findings: The studies find that observers expect only high-status (HS) individuals to engage in and succeed at moralistic punishment of norm violations. HS punishers are seen as more likely to be successful, more dominant, and more likely to be chosen for leadership roles. Low-status (LS) individuals are not expected to punish, and even if they do, they do not gain the same reputational or leadership benefits. The perceived benefits of punishment (e.g., increased likability, dominance, or leadership nomination) are conditional on both the status of the punisher and the success of the intervention. The work does not address actual behavioral outcomes in economic games, nor does it report on efficiency or payoff effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding social expectations and reputational dynamics around punishment, especially the role of status, but do not inform quantitative predictions about group efficiency or payoff outcomes when punishment is enabled. The results may be contextually useful for interpreting when and why punishment is expected or effective in real-world or experimental settings, particularly regarding heterogeneity in punishment costs or effectiveness due to status, but they do not provide data on efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Gordon, D. S., Madden, J. R., & Lea, S. E. G. (2014). Both Loved and Feared: Third Party Punishers Are Viewed as Formidable and Likeable, but These Reputational Benefits May Only Be Open to Dominant Individuals. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that third party punishment is perceived as both a dominant and likable act, distinguishing it from other aggressive behaviors. However, only dominant individuals are seen as able to punish effectively and at low cost, with reduced risk of retaliation and greater ability to use non-violent (social) punishment. Subordinate individuals are perceived as less likely to succeed and at higher risk if they attempt punishment. The reputational benefits of punishment (increased likability and dominance) are thus mainly accessible to dominant individuals. These findings are based on observer judgments in hypothetical scenarios, not on actual behavior in economic games or real payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, suggest that heterogeneity in the cost and effectiveness of punishment (due to dominance/status) may be important moderators of the impact of punishment on cooperation and norm enforcement. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that the effectiveness and social acceptability of punishment may depend on the perceived status of the punisher, but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental data on efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are relevant for understanding mechanisms and moderators of punishment, but not for direct prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Adams, G. S., & Mullen, E. (2013). Increased Voting for Candidates Who Compensate Victims Rather than Punish Offenders. *Social Justice Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that people prefer to vote for candidates who compensate victims (or both compensate and punish) over those who only punish perpetrators or do not respond to injustice. This preference is robust across different types of transgressions (genocide, bike theft, domestic violence) and is mediated by perceptions of warmth. Perceived effectiveness of the response is measured subjectively, not as a monetary or group payoff. The prevalence of the transgression and political orientation do not moderate these effects. The studies do not involve economic games, group cooperation, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar economic games. The outcomes are about third-party voting preferences and trait perceptions, not about how punishment affects cooperation, contributions, or efficiency. While the paper discusses punishment and compensation as responses to norm violations, it does not manipulate or measure the game design dimensions relevant to public goods games, nor does it report any payoff-based outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Zhang, Y., & van der Schaar, M. (2013). Robust Reputation Protocol Design for Online Communities: A Stochastic Stability Analysis. *Ieee Journal of Selected Topics in Signal Processing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in a repeated random matching game (asymmetric gift-giving, not a standard PGG), a reputation protocol with probabilistic punishment (parameterized by punishment probability beta) can induce full cooperation and achieve maximal efficiency (social welfare = b-c) in the long run, provided the discount factor and punishment strength are within certain bounds. If punishment is too weak or too strong, efficiency is lower. Theoretical analysis is supported by numerical simulations showing that the optimal protocol achieves much higher average social welfare than direct reciprocity (tit-for-tat) or less robust reputation schemes. The protocol's design is robust to stochastic perturbations and ensures convergence to the efficient equilibrium.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated random matching environments with binary contribution (gift-giving), the introduction of a well-calibrated reputation-based punishment protocol can move the system from zero efficiency (no cooperation) to full efficiency (all cooperate, social welfare maximized). The effect is robust to noise and initial conditions, provided the punishment probability is neither too low nor too high. While the game is not a standard PGG, the adjacent structure and explicit efficiency analysis make the results highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar environments. The findings suggest that, when punishment is enabled via a reputation protocol and properly tuned, efficiency can increase dramatically compared to the no-punishment baseline.

- source: Normann, H. T., Rösch, J., & Schultz, L. M. (2015). Do buyer groups facilitate collusion?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: In repeated Cournot triopoly experiments, buyer groups that can exclude members (closed buyer groups) enable targeted punishment of high-output (deviating) firms, which facilitates collusion and reduces industry output toward the joint-profit maximizing (collusive) level. This reduces efficiency and welfare compared to baseline or open buyer groups (which cannot exclude). Communication, especially when used for explicit quantitative agreements, further increases collusion and reduces output. Open buyer groups without exclusion do not significantly differ from baseline in terms of output or efficiency. The exclusion mechanism is used mainly against high-output firms, consistent with punishment of deviations. Overall, mechanisms that enable punishment (exclusion) or communication (especially explicit agreements) reduce efficiency by facilitating collusion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling targeted punishment (via exclusion from a buyer group) in a repeated Cournot game reduces efficiency by facilitating collusion, as measured by lower industry output and welfare. The effect is more pronounced when communication is allowed, especially with explicit quantitative agreements. In the absence of exclusion or communication, buyer groups do not significantly affect efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (exclusion), communication, and the structure of the game (repeated, triopoly, continuous output) are key moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment. The findings are directly relevant for settings where punishment is implemented as exclusion or similar targeted sanctions, and less so for standard PGGs with costly punishment tokens.

- source: Yuan, Y., Wang, F. Y., & Zeng, D. (2016). Developing a cooperative bidding framework for sponsored search markets - An evolutionary perspective. *Information Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated sponsored search auctions, if advertisers are limited to unconditional strategies (always cooperate or always compete), competitive strategies dominate, resulting in high bids and low efficiency (advertiser revenue is about 0.64 times the UBNE outcome, which is suboptimal). When contingent 'nice and retaliatory' (NR) strategies are available—where advertisers cooperate unless a rival deviates, in which case they punish by bidding competitively—stable cooperation emerges. This leads to lower bids (at the GEAE, the lowest-price equilibrium) and advertiser revenues at the socially optimal VCG level. The NR strategies are neutrally evolutionarily stable: they cannot be strongly invaded by competitive strategies, and cooperation persists. The effect is robust to incomplete information, as long as advertisers can monitor rivals' bids and retaliate. The findings are supported by both theoretical analysis and coevolutionary simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment-like contingent strategies (NR) in repeated, multi-player, auction-like public goods environments can substantially increase efficiency (group payoff) relative to a control condition where only unconditional strategies are available. The effect is robust across a range of parameterizations (number of players, rounds, etc.), and the mechanism is directly analogous to punishment in public goods games: punishment of defectors stabilizes cooperation and increases group welfare. For prediction tasks, the presence of contingent punishment strategies should be expected to increase efficiency from a low baseline (competitive equilibrium) to near the social optimum, provided players can observe and respond to others' actions. The results are most directly applicable to repeated, multi-player, auction-like games with observable actions and the possibility of contingent retaliation.

- source: Lumeau, M., Masclet, D., & Penard, T. (2015). Reputation and social (dis)approval in feedback mechanisms: An experimental study. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both private and public feedback mechanisms increase trust (amount sent) and trustworthiness (return rate) in a repeated trust game compared to a baseline with no feedback. Public feedback (where ratings are observable to future partners) is more effective than private feedback (where ratings are only seen by the recipient) in increasing these behaviors. The difference is attributed to both a higher frequency of ratings and a stronger behavioral response to public ratings. When a cost is introduced for assigning ratings, the effects persist but are weaker, especially for private feedback. The study concludes that reputation mechanisms (public feedback) are essential for sustaining high levels of cooperation and implied efficiency in environments with imperfect information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a feedback mechanism (a form of non-monetary sanction or reward) increases cooperation and implied efficiency in trust-game-like environments. Publicly observable feedback (reputation) is more effective than private feedback (social (dis)approval only). The results are robust to the introduction of a small cost for assigning feedback. For prediction tasks, the presence and observability of feedback mechanisms should be expected to increase efficiency, with public/reputational mechanisms having a larger effect than private ones. However, the results are from a trust game, not a PGG, so direct quantitative transfer to PGGs should be cautious, but the qualitative direction is likely similar.

- source: Gervais, M. M. (2017). RICH Economic Games for Networked Relationships and Communities: Development and Preliminary Validation in Yasawa, Fiji. *Field Methods*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a Fijian village, participants are willing to spend significant portions of their endowment to reduce the earnings of others in a costly reduction game (CRG), with 66% of deciders reducing at least one target. The mean amount spent on reductions was 45% of the available stake. Target earnings varied widely, and some participants ended with negative aggregate earnings (before post-hoc adjustment). The motives for reduction included both punitive and leveling (egalitarian) reasons. Compared to anonymous-target punishment games in neighboring villages, the rate of punitive behavior is higher in the CRG, likely due to the integration of recipient identities. However, the study does not directly measure the effect of punishment on group efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline, nor does it use a standard public goods game structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a context where punishment is possible and recipient identities are known, individuals are willing to pay substantial costs to reduce others' payoffs, often for leveling or punitive reasons. The cost-to-impact ratio is clearly specified (0.5 FJ$ cost to reduce 2 FJ$ from a target). However, because the game is not a standard public goods game and there is no control condition without punishment, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. The high willingness to punish and the resulting wide variation in earnings suggest that punishment can lead to significant reductions in aggregate payoffs, especially when used for leveling rather than norm enforcement. The study is most informative for settings where punishment is possible, costly, and can be used for reasons beyond norm enforcement, particularly in small, close-knit communities.

- source: Jung, Y. B., Hall, J., Hong, R. Y., Goh, T., Ong, N., & Tan, N. (2014). Payback: Effects of relationship and cultural norms on reciprocity. *Asian Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that reciprocity norms (reward and punishment) are shaped by relationship and culture. Collectivists are more generous to friends after positive treatment and less likely to punish after negative treatment (even to strangers), sometimes offering an 'olive branch' (positive or neutral response to negative treatment). Individualists are more likely to punish norm violators (destroy money at a cost to themselves) regardless of relationship. The main dependent variable is the amount given or destroyed, not group efficiency or total payoff. The study does not use a public goods game or a repeated multi-player setting, but rather a dyadic hybrid trust-dictator game with a single round (though participants believed there would be five rounds).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the behavioral drivers of punishment and reward in dyadic trust/dictator game settings, especially the influence of culture and relationship on willingness to punish at a cost. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game or multi-player repeated setting. The findings may inform expectations about the likelihood and intensity of costly punishment or reward in similar dyadic games, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency changes from enabling punishment in public goods games. The evidence is most relevant for understanding the psychological and cultural moderators of punishment behavior, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Safarzynska, K. (2013). The coevolution of culture and environment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that costly resource conservation (including punishment of defectors) can evolve in structured populations facing renewable resource constraints and environmental shocks. Groups with many defectors are more likely to overharvest and collapse, while groups with more cooperators and punishers are more resilient. Punishment reduces the within-group advantage of defection, but is itself costly. Environmental shocks (weather) accelerate the elimination of non-cooperative groups. Between-group interactions (resource-conflict and harvest-sharing) modulate the evolution of cooperation: resource-conflict increases the frequency of resource conservation by increasing collapse of defector-heavy groups, while harvest-sharing reduces group collapse and allows more defection. Larger group size has a non-monotonic effect: above a threshold, larger groups are more likely to collapse, which can favor the evolution of conservation. Policies that increase the cost of extraction or penalize defectors promote conservation. However, the model does not report group efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome, focusing instead on behavioral strategy frequencies and group survival.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that punishment and resource conservation can evolve and persist in group-structured resource dilemmas, especially under environmental variability and when group collapse is possible. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The model's outcomes are mainly about the frequency of cooperative/punishing strategies and group survival, not efficiency as defined for the prediction task. Thus, while the mechanisms discussed (e.g., punishment reducing defection, group collapse selecting for cooperation) are relevant for understanding when punishment might improve group outcomes, the paper does not provide data or results that can be directly mapped to efficiency predictions for specific game design dimensions.

- source: Li, Q., Iqbal, A., Perc, M., Chen, M. Y., & Abbott, D. (2013). Coevolution of Quantum and Classical Strategies on Evolving Random Networks. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when agents can punish by reducing link weights, cooperation can dominate for a wider range of temptation values, especially when the network structure is updated frequently. Stronger punishment (lower minimum link weight) increases the range where cooperation dominates. As the temptation to defect increases, quantum strategies eventually dominate. The number of neighbors (network degree) has little effect if all agents have equal degree. The network evolves to have higher clustering and small-world properties. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the findings are about strategy prevalence, not direct payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (reducing link weights) on the prevalence of cooperation in an evolving network Prisoner's Dilemma. It suggests that stronger punishment and more frequent network updating support cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only adjacent: it supports the idea that punishment can sustain cooperation under some conditions, but does not quantify efficiency gains or losses. The game is not a public goods game, but a networked PD with quantum strategies, so transfer to PGG-like settings should be cautious.

- source: Gingins, S., Werminghausen, J., Johnstone, R. A., Grutter, A. S., & Bshary, R. (2013). Power and temptation cause shifts between exploitation and cooperation in a cleaner wrasse mutualism. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_tech
  findings: Cleaner wrasse strategically adjust their foraging behavior in response to the threat of early termination (sanction) and temptation to cheat. When the threat of early termination is high and the temptation to cheat is low, cleaners behave more cooperatively (eat more less-preferred items, which is analogous to providing better service). When the temptation to cheat is high and the threat is low, cleaners exploit the situation (eat more preferred items). The non-cleaner control species does not adjust its behavior in response to these manipulations. The study provides evidence that simple partner control mechanisms (sanctions) can maintain cooperation in asymmetric interactions, but does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that the presence of a sanction mechanism (threat of early termination) can shift behavior from exploitation to cooperation, especially when the temptation to cheat is low. However, the outcomes are behavioral and not directly about efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that, in analogous public goods games, enabling a sanction mechanism may increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency would depend on the cost structure and whether the sanction is costly or not. The study is most informative for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which sanctions can promote cooperation, but less directly informative for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Mameli, M. (2013). Meat made us moral: a hypothesis on the nature and evolution of moral judgment. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that the evolution of moral judgment is rooted in emotional dispositions that support the enforcement of group-beneficial norms through punishment and meta-punishment. It draws on ethnographic and evolutionary accounts of hunter-gatherer societies, where collective punishment of cheaters and bullies (those who disrupt cooperation and resource sharing) is common and is enforced through both direct and reputational means (e.g., gossip, ostracism, disapproval). The author suggests that these mechanisms enabled stable cooperation in small groups, but the analysis is conceptual and evolutionary rather than empirical or quantitative. The paper does not provide direct evidence or predictions about efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment and meta-punishment mechanisms can stabilize cooperation in small, egalitarian groups, especially in contexts analogous to public goods dilemmas (e.g., meat sharing after cooperative hunting). However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct predictions about efficiency or group payoff outcomes with or without punishment. Its relevance is mainly in supporting the plausibility of punishment as a stabilizer of cooperation, especially when punishment is collective, reputational, and embedded in group norms. For prediction tasks, this paper may inform the expected direction of punishment effects in small, highly interactive, reputation-sensitive groups, but it does not provide quantitative or design-specific guidance.

- source: Lacomba, J. A., Lagos, F., Reuben, E., & van Winden, F. (2014). On the escalation and de-escalation of conflict. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in one-shot or randomly matched (Strangers) conflict games, conflict expenditures are high and escalate over time, leading to low efficiency (mean earnings well below the endowment). When losers can destroy resources (akin to punishment), conflict expenditures decrease, and in the 'Resistance' game (where destruction can be conditioned on the winner's appropriation), mean earnings are significantly higher than in the baseline. However, destruction itself is wasteful and only increases efficiency if it deters appropriation and reduces conflict. In repeated interaction (Partners), a substantial minority of groups achieve long-lasting peace (zero conflict, high efficiency), but most do not. Peace is achieved through costly signaling (not fighting, low appropriation), not through destruction. Overall, the effect of punishment-like destruction on efficiency is mixed: it can increase efficiency by reducing conflict, but can also reduce it if destruction is frequent. The design dimensions (matching protocol, post-conflict options) strongly moderate these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, the ability of losers to destroy resources) on efficiency in conflict games is highly context-dependent. In one-shot or random-matching settings, allowing punishment (destruction) can reduce conflict expenditures and, if destruction is conditional on appropriation, can increase efficiency. However, if destruction is frequent, efficiency may not improve. In repeated games, the possibility of peace (high efficiency) depends more on costly signaling and restraint than on punishment. For prediction, the presence of a punishment mechanism (destruction) is likely to reduce conflict and increase efficiency only if it credibly deters appropriation and is not overused. The matching protocol (repeated vs. random) and the structure of post-conflict options are key moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) is low in these games; enabling punishment can increase efficiency in some variants (notably Resistance/RE), but not always.

- source: Barker, J. L., & Barclay, P. (2016). Local competition increases people's willingness to harm others. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper demonstrates that when competition is local (i.e., participants compete for prizes within small groups), people are more willing to pay a cost to harm others, resulting in lower group and individual payoffs (efficiency). In contrast, under global competition (competing against all participants), less money is spent on harming, and payoffs are higher. This effect holds whether endowments are earned or given. The findings are robust across three studies, including one where participants could harm both ingroup and outgroup members: under local competition, harming is targeted at ingroup members (the direct competitors), further reducing within-group efficiency. The results are interpreted as a 'tragedy of the commons' driven by local competition, where wasteful harming reduces overall welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in economic games where players can pay to reduce others' payoffs (money-burning), the structure of competition (local vs global) is a critical moderator of efficiency. When competition is local, efficiency is much lower due to increased costly harming, even when the cost-effectiveness of harming is high (burning $1 to reduce $4). The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of local competition (small group contests) should be expected to strongly reduce efficiency when punishment/harming is possible, compared to global competition. The results are robust to whether endowments are earned or given, and to whether harming is targeted at ingroup or outgroup members. However, the game is not a standard public goods game or punishment game: the main action is costly harming, not punishment for norm violation or contribution decisions. Still, the efficiency implications are directly relevant for settings where costly sanctions are possible.

- source: Camera, G., & Casari, M. (2014). The Coordination Value of Monetary Exchange: Experimental Evidence. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated, anonymous helping game with four players, introducing a fiat money-like mechanism (tickets) does not increase the maximum attainable efficiency but changes how cooperation is coordinated. In the baseline (no tickets), realized efficiency is 48.2% of the maximum. With tickets, overall efficiency is 46.8%, not significantly different from baseline. However, when ticket transfer is feasible, cooperation and efficiency rise to 61.4%; when not feasible, they drop to 12.5%. The presence of tickets leads to the emergence of a monetary exchange strategy, increases the predictability of cooperative behavior, and redistributes surplus from frequent defectors to frequent cooperators. The main effect of the monetary mechanism is to facilitate coordination and reduce strategic uncertainty, not to increase aggregate efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing a monetary-like mechanism (tickets) in a repeated, anonymous, decentralized helping game does not increase overall efficiency compared to a baseline with only decentralized punishment, but it does change the distribution and predictability of cooperation. The effect of the monetary mechanism is highly contingent on whether the mechanism is feasible in a given match. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence of a monetary or token-based exchange mechanism may not increase group efficiency if the baseline already allows for decentralized punishment and the efficiency frontier is not expanded. However, such mechanisms can facilitate coordination, increase the stability of cooperation, and redistribute surplus toward cooperators. The results are most relevant for environments where punishment is decentralized and impersonal, and where direct reciprocity and reputation are absent.

- source: Abdulkadiroglu, A., & Bagwell, K. (2013). Trust, Reciprocity, and Favors in Cooperative Relationships. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated trust games with private information, efficiency (total group payoff) can be increased above the autarky (Nash) level through favor-exchange relationships (dynamic reciprocity) or through symmetric punishment (negative reciprocity). The optimal equilibrium structure depends on the parameters: when information asymmetries are low, symmetric punishment can support higher efficiency than favor-exchange. Hybrid equilibria (honeymoon period followed by favor-exchange or punishment) can further improve efficiency in some cases. The paper provides explicit formulas for the efficiency achieved under each regime. Immediate reciprocity (punishment for non-reciprocation) is not always necessary; dynamic reciprocity (future favor) is often sufficient. The model is not a PGG but is closely related in its focus on cooperation, trust, and efficiency under repeated interaction and private information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical predictions for how efficiency in repeated cooperation games is affected by the availability and structure of punishment and favor-exchange mechanisms. While not a PGG, the model is adjacent and the efficiency formulas and comparative statics are informative for predicting efficiency in environments where punishment (symmetric, infrequent) is possible versus when only favor-exchange is available. The results suggest that, in some parameter regions, introducing symmetric punishment can increase efficiency above favor-exchange alone, especially when information asymmetries are low. However, the model is limited to two players and does not include standard PGG features like group size or marginal per-capita return. The findings are most useful for understanding the theoretical potential of punishment to enhance efficiency in repeated cooperation with private information, but direct quantitative prediction for PGGs would require caution.

- source: Shibayama, S. (2015). Academic commercialization and changing nature of academic cooperation. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that increasing the prevalence of commercial actors (who do not cooperate gratis) in an academic community undermines the reputation mechanism that sustains indirect reciprocity. This leads to a decline in unconditional cooperation, a rise in demand for direct rewards (e.g., coauthorship, barter), and an increase in abstention from cooperation. The overall effect is a reduction in group efficiency and mean payoff, as the system moves away from the collectively optimal state. Centralized rewarding can partially restore cooperation and efficiency, but only if the reward is carefully calibrated; excessive rewards can backfire by incentivizing defectors. The model provides formal predictions and phase diagrams showing how the basin of attraction for efficient cooperation shrinks as commercialization increases.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence and design of punishment, reward, and reputation mechanisms are critical moderators of efficiency in public-goods-like environments. While not a direct PGG or lab experiment, the model is closely related and analyzes efficiency outcomes as a function of game design parameters (e.g., cost/benefit of cooperation, reward size, reputation availability, prevalence of defectors). For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling effective punishment or reward (especially centralized, carefully sized rewards) can increase efficiency, but that the presence of many defectors or commercial actors will reduce the effectiveness of reputation-based cooperation and lower efficiency. The model also highlights the importance of the matching rate for rewards and the availability of reputation information. However, the absence of empirical or experimental data means that quantitative predictions should be made with caution.

- source: Al-Dhanhani, A., Mizouni, R., Otrok, H., & Al-Rubaie, A. (2014). A game theoretical model for collaborative groups in social applications. *Expert Systems with Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of reciprocity and punishment strategies in repeated social dilemmas (modeled as asynchronous iterated prisoner's dilemma) has a strong effect on group efficiency and the distribution of gains between cooperative and selfish (free riding) users. Classical Tit for Tat leads to deadlock and declining payoffs. Generous Tit for Tat avoids deadlock and increases group gain, but allows free riders to benefit disproportionately. Reputation-based Tit for Tat reduces free rider gain but can also reduce cooperative user gain, especially if the reputation threshold is set high or the initialization phase is short. Group reputation-based Tit for Tat (indirect reciprocity) achieves the highest cooperative user gain and lowest free rider gain, thus maximizing group efficiency and survivability. The presence of free riders always reduces group efficiency, but the right punishment/reciprocity mechanism can mitigate this loss.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of punishment/reciprocity mechanisms (especially those based on group reputation and indirect reciprocity) can significantly improve group efficiency in repeated social dilemma environments, even in the presence of free riders. While the model is not a standard public goods game, it is closely related and the efficiency outcomes are directly comparable. The findings suggest that enabling punishment or exclusion based on group-level reputation (rather than only direct reciprocity) is likely to increase efficiency relative to no punishment or to simple direct reciprocity. The results are most relevant for settings with repeated interactions, observable histories, and the ability to track and act on group-level reputation. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or direct mappings to all design dimensions, but the qualitative direction is clear: more sophisticated, group-based punishment/reciprocity mechanisms are more effective at sustaining efficiency.

- source: Sasaki, T., Okada, I., & Nakai, Y. (2016). Indirect reciprocity can overcome free-rider problems on costly moral assessment. *Biology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity models with costly assessment, cooperation is not stable unless there is a mechanism to sanction or exclude those who evade the cost of assessment. By introducing a pre-assessment stage that labels non-payers as 'nasty' and excludes them from receiving help, the model shows that a cooperative equilibrium (where everyone pays for assessment and helps others) can be locally stable if the benefit of helping exceeds the cost of assessment. This mechanism is analogous to pool punishment in public goods games and is effective under leading social norms. The findings are theoretical but directly address efficiency and the conditions for stable cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that institutional mechanisms to sanction or exclude cost-evaders (such as pre-assessment or pool punishment) can significantly improve efficiency in social dilemma games with costly monitoring or punishment. For prediction tasks, the presence of a pre-assessment or institutionalized punishment mechanism is likely to shift efficiency from low (collapse of cooperation) to high (stable cooperation), provided the net benefit of cooperation is positive. The results are robust across different social norms and suggest that the design of the punishment/sanctioning mechanism is a key moderator of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Martinez-Vaquero, L. A., Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., & Lenaerts, T. (2017). When agreement-accepting free-riders are a necessary evil for the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in one-shot games, the coexistence of proposers and acceptors can be necessary to keep defectors out of the population, especially when the cost of commitment must be paid before knowing if the other will accept (prior commitments). In repeated games, proposers alone can sustain cooperation, and the presence of acceptors can actually reduce cooperation by making it easier for defectors to invade. The introduction of an apology-forgiveness mechanism (where apologies after defection can restore cooperation) can be exploited by acceptors if the apology cost is too low, leading to lower cooperation. The results are framed in terms of evolutionary stability and the stationary distribution of strategies, with average payoff as the fitness criterion, but do not directly report group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but it does offer adjacent theoretical insights about how commitment mechanisms (which can be seen as a form of pre-emptive contract or compensation for defection) affect the prevalence of cooperation and the exclusion of defectors. The findings suggest that the design of commitment mechanisms (timing of costs, ability to recognize intentions, presence of apology-forgiveness) can strongly moderate the success of cooperation-promoting strategies and thus the average payoff in the population. However, because the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma and not a public goods game, and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning as in PGGs, its direct predictive value for the downstream task is limited. The results are most relevant for understanding how alternative mechanisms to punishment (commitments, compensation, apology) might affect cooperation and payoff in related social dilemmas.

- source: Angourakis, A., Santos, J. I., Galán, J. M., & Balbo, A. L. (2015). Food for all: An agent-based model to explore the emergence and implications of cooperation for food storage. *Environmental Archaeology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The model demonstrates that cooperation in food storage is most likely to emerge and persist when (1) cooperative storage is at least as efficient as household storage, (2) there is moderate to high dependency on storable food, and (3) social norms regulating access to the cooperative stock are neither too strict nor too lax. Too strict enforcement (high reciprocity requirement) can undermine cooperation by excluding agents who fail to contribute due to bad luck, while too lax enforcement allows free-riding to undermine the common stock. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff in the public goods sense, but shows that cooperation increases the probability of avoiding shortages and, under certain conditions, can lead to surplus. The findings suggest that moderate sanctioning (via access control) is optimal for sustaining cooperation in common-pool resource settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff in a public goods game with explicit punishment, it models a closely related common-pool resource dilemma where access to the common resource is conditional on past cooperation (a form of sanction). The results suggest that intermediate levels of sanctioning (not too strict, not too lax) maximize cooperation and system performance (in terms of shortage avoidance and potential surplus). For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment or exclusion mechanisms that are too harsh may reduce efficiency by discouraging recovery from bad luck, while too little enforcement allows free-riding. The model also highlights the importance of storage efficiency and dependency on the resource, which may map onto MPCR and baseline efficiency in public goods games. However, direct quantitative predictions of efficiency with and without punishment are not available from this model.

- source: Nakashima, N. A., Halali, E., & Halevy, N. (2017). Third parties promote cooperative norms in repeated interactions. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Third party intervention in repeated adversarial interactions (modeled as a Peacemaker Game) increases cooperation rates among adversaries. Early intervention is more effective at establishing persistent cooperative norms than late intervention. Third parties are more likely to intervene when adversaries have a history of cooperation, and adversaries are more likely to cooperate following third party intervention. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation and intervention rates), not efficiency or group payoff. The study provides indirect evidence that mechanisms which combine reward and punishment can transform competitive repeated interactions into more cooperative ones, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that enabling third-party interventions (which combine reward and punishment) can increase cooperation in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-like settings. However, because the main outcomes are cooperation and intervention rates, not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as suggestive rather than directly predictive for efficiency outcomes. The game structure is adjacent to public goods games, and the intervention mechanism is not a standard peer punishment or reward system. The results suggest that early, consistent intervention is more effective at establishing cooperation than late intervention, and that the presence of a third party who can both reward and punish can transform group behavior. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that enabling such mechanisms is likely to increase cooperation, but the quantitative effect on efficiency is not directly reported.

- source: Halevy, N., & Halali, E. (2015). Selfish third parties act as peacemakers by transforming conflicts and promoting cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main findings are: (1) The possibility and higher probability of third-party intervention increase cooperation rates among disputants in both interpersonal and intergroup contexts. (2) Third parties' willingness to intervene is highly sensitive to the risk and potential rewards of intervention; reducing risk or increasing potential gain increases intervention rates. (3) Despite the potential for increased collective gains, third parties often intervene less frequently than would be optimal, leading to missed opportunities for higher group payoffs. (4) The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as primary outcomes, but discusses them in the context of missed opportunities and the structure of the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the introduction of a punishment/reward mechanism (via third-party intervention) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma-like settings, even when the underlying game is not a standard public goods game. However, because the primary outcomes are behavioral (cooperation/intervention rates) and not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that the design of punishment/reward mechanisms (including the risk and incentive structure for enforcers) is critical for their effectiveness. The evidence is most relevant for adjacent settings where third-party intervention is possible, but less directly transferable to standard PGGs with endogenous peer punishment.

- source: Cason, T. N., & Mui, V. L. (2014). Coordinating Resistance Through Communication and Repeated Interaction. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that repetition alone only modestly reduces leader DAC (transgression) rates, as leaders can punish beneficiaries who resist, deterring future resistance. Communication between responders, even when non-binding and private, substantially increases the rate of coordinated resistance and reduces leader DAC. The combination of repetition and communication is most effective at reducing DAC. The study also shows that hiding responder identities from the leader (so the leader cannot target challenging beneficiaries) increases resistance. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total surplus, or group payoff as a main outcome, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured. The results are most relevant for understanding how communication and repeated interaction affect coordination in social dilemmas with endogenous roles and targeted punishment, rather than standard public goods games with peer punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the importance of communication and repeated interaction for coordinated resistance in social dilemmas with endogenous roles. While not a public goods game, the findings suggest that communication between potential punishers (or resisters) can be as important as, or more important than, repetition for deterring exploitative behavior. The results highlight that the ability to target punishers (e.g., identifying who punished) can undermine the effectiveness of resistance. For prediction tasks in public goods games, the main lesson is that communication channels and anonymity of punishment/reward can strongly moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation and, by extension, efficiency. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes, but it does suggest that communication is a powerful moderator.

- source: Bednarik, P., Fehl, K., & Semmann, D. (2014). Costs for switching partners reduce network dynamics but not cooperative behaviour. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing costs for forming new links in dynamic networks reduces the rate at which participants break links (i.e., switch partners).
