However, cooperation rates in the repeated Prisoner's Dilemma remain high across all dynamic treatments, regardless of the cost level, and are significantly higher than in static networks where no partner switching is allowed. The ability to break links—even if rarely used—appears to be enough to maintain high cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of these dynamics on efficiency cannot be directly determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with dynamic partner networks, the introduction of costs for switching partners reduces network dynamism but does not reduce cooperation rates. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment or partner-switching mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings suggest that the mere threat of partner switching can sustain cooperation, but the efficiency implications (e.g., total group payoff relative to maximum possible) remain unaddressed.

- source: Harth, N. S., & Regner, T. (2017). The spiral of distrust: (Non-)cooperation in a repeated trust game is predicted by anger and individual differences in negative reciprocity orientation. *International Journal of Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that senders who receive feedback about low back transfers from trustees experience increased anger, which in turn leads to reduced cooperation (lower amounts sent) in subsequent rounds. Individuals with a high negative reciprocity orientation are more prone to anger and are less willing to cooperate, even in the absence of anger-eliciting feedback. The interaction between anger and negative reciprocity orientation further reduces cooperation. The absence of feedback results in lower anger and relatively higher cooperation, especially among those low in negative reciprocity orientation. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it studies a repeated trust game without any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral (amount sent/cooperation) and emotional (anger), not payoff-based. However, it provides indirect evidence that negative emotions and individual differences in reciprocity orientation can reduce cooperation in repeated interactions, especially when feedback about others' behavior is available. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only weakly informative and should not be weighted heavily unless the prediction context is specifically about trust games or the psychological mechanisms of cooperation.

- source: Zhen, S. S., & Yu, R. J. (2016). Tend to Compare and Tend to Be Fair: The Relationship between Social Comparison Sensitivity and Justice Sensitivity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds a positive correlation between social comparison sensitivity and justice sensitivity, both in behavioral tasks (UG and SCG) and in self-report inventories. In the UG, participants are more likely to reject disadvantageous offers, and this tendency is associated with their behavior in the SCG. The results suggest that social comparison motives underlie fairness-related decisions, particularly the rejection of unfair offers. However, the study does not include a public goods game, does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a separate stage, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses a modified ultimatum game (dyadic, proposer-responder) and a social comparison game, focusing on individual differences in fairness and comparison sensitivity. There is no group interaction, no punishment stage as in PGGs, and no efficiency or group payoff data. The findings may be relevant for understanding motives behind rejection (which can be interpreted as a form of costly punishment), but the lack of group-level payoff outcomes and absence of a PGG or punishment-enabled/control design means this paper offers only indirect, limited support for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Jones, M. T. (2014). Strategic complexity and cooperation: An experimental study. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Increasing the complexity of strategies required to sustain cooperation in an indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma reduces the frequency of cooperation and increases the prevalence of simple selfish strategies. This effect is observed both when the cooperate action and when the defect action are made state-dependent, increasing the information processing required for cooperation or punishment, respectively. The study also finds that higher cognitive ability is associated with greater cooperation in the baseline, but this advantage is reduced when complexity is increased. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral measures such as cooperation rates and strategy inference.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that increasing the cognitive or strategic complexity of sustaining cooperation in repeated social dilemmas reduces cooperation rates, and by implication, would likely reduce efficiency or group payoff if measured. However, the study is not a public goods game and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, it relies on endogenous punishment via repeated game strategies (e.g., Grim Trigger). The findings are most relevant for predicting how complexity or cognitive burden might moderate the effect of punishment or cooperation-supporting mechanisms in repeated games, but do not provide direct evidence for the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games. Use this paper as informative indirect evidence about the role of complexity and cognitive ability in sustaining cooperation, but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Frey, V., & de Rijt, A. V. (2016). Arbitrary Inequality in Reputation Systems. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when trustors can observe the outcomes of others' exchanges (information sharing), they are more likely to repeatedly select the same trustee, leading to 'reputation cascades' and increasing inequality in how often trustees are chosen. This effect is strongest when there is a risk of trust abuse (the 'trust problem' condition). The paper shows that these cascades are arbitrary, as trustees with similar trustworthiness can end up with very different exchange volumes. While the paper discusses payoffs in the game structure, it does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. The main focus is on behavioral dynamics of trust and reputation, not on efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of information sharing and reputation systems on behavioral outcomes in trust-based exchange games. It does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods or punishment games, as it does not report these outcomes. However, it suggests that increased information sharing can increase trust and exchange, but also leads to arbitrary inequality in participation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, as the game structure and outcomes differ from standard PGGs and do not include punishment or efficiency as primary outcomes.

  findings: Across three experiments, the authors show that (1) making moral judgments of others' immoral behavior increases subsequent trustworthiness in a trust game (measured by amount returned), (2) this effect is mediated by increased self-perceived moral identity, and (3) observers trust and prefer to interact with strong moral judges, perceiving them as more moral and trustworthy. The behavioral outcomes are individual-level trust and trustworthiness, not group-level efficiency or payoff. The design does not include public goods games, explicit punishment, or sanctioning mechanisms.

However, there is no manipulation of punishment, no public goods game, and no group payoff or efficiency outcome. The results may be relevant for understanding how non-material social judgments can affect prosocial behavior, but they do not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: McNally, L., & Jackson, A. L. (2013). Cooperation creates selection for tactical deception. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that conditional cooperation mechanisms (including punishment and reward) can select for the evolution of tactical deception, as deceivers can exploit conditional cooperators by hiding their defection. Theoretical results show that, under certain conditions (notably negative frequency dependence in deception detection), cooperation and deception can coexist at equilibrium. Comparative analysis in primates supports the prediction that more cooperative species exhibit more tactical deception. However, the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, nor does it experimentally manipulate punishment or public goods game parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its theoretical model is based on the iterated prisoner's dilemma and focuses on the evolutionary dynamics of deception and cooperation, not on payoff or efficiency outcomes. While it discusses punishment as one of several conditional cooperation mechanisms, it does not analyze the quantitative impact of punishment on group efficiency or payoff. Therefore, it offers only indirect, contextual support for the idea that enforcement mechanisms can have complex evolutionary consequences, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.

- source: Sterelny, K. (2016). Cooperation, Culture, and Conflict. *British Journal for the Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that human cooperation evolved in two major transitions: first, among Pleistocene foragers, where cooperation was individually advantageous and stabilized by mutualism, reciprocation, and punishment of freeriders (both shirkers and bullies); and second, in the Holocene, where larger, more hierarchical societies required new mechanisms (including group selection and intergroup conflict) to maintain cooperation. Punishment is discussed as a key mechanism for controlling freeriding and maintaining cooperation, but the analysis is conceptual and evolutionary rather than experimental or quantitative. The paper references experimental public goods games and their findings (e.g., punishment stabilizes cooperation), but only as background for theoretical arguments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how punishment and norm enforcement may affect cooperation and, by extension, efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions. However, it supports the idea that punishment is more likely to stabilize cooperation (and thus efficiency) in small, transparent, repeated-interaction groups, and that the effectiveness of punishment may depend on social structure, group size, and transparency. For prediction tasks, this paper is best used as background for mechanism hypotheses rather than as a source of direct predictive evidence.

- source: Fagundes, D. (2017). The Social Norms of Waiting in Line. *Law and Social Inquiry-journal of the American Bar Foundation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that the stability of waiting lines among strangers is best explained by the human tendency toward strong reciprocity: people are inclined to cooperate when they see others doing so and to punish norm violators, even at personal cost. It draws on public goods game experiments to support this model, noting that people will punish defectors even when it is costly. The paper describes how informal sanctions (verbal objections, shaming, and sometimes violence) deter line-cutting and maintain order. It also discusses cultural and contextual variation in queue norms, exceptions to the rules, and the role of service providers and technology. However, the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or group payoffs, nor does it manipulate or systematically analyze game design dimensions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for the role of punishment and social norms in sustaining cooperation in environments analogous to public goods games, such as physical queues. It supports the idea that informal sanctions can deter defection and promote norm compliance, even among strangers. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper may inform mechanistic reasoning about why punishment can sustain cooperation, but it does not provide empirical data or parameter estimates relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions.

The authors argue that such experiments can help determine whether normative principles are psychologically realistic and can be implemented in practice. However, the paper does not present new experimental data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes directly.

However, it does not provide quantitative or design-specific evidence that would inform predictions about efficiency changes under different game design dimensions.

- source: Johnson, D. D. P., Price, M. E., & Van Vugt, M. (2013). Darwin's invisible hand: Market competition, evolution and the firm. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper proposes that the standard economic view of market competition as a Darwinian process is incomplete, because it overlooks the group-level selection that occurs among firms. Using multi-level selection theory, the authors argue that both individual and group selection pressures shape behavior in firms, leading to a mix of self-interested and group-beneficial behaviors. The paper draws on existing empirical literature (including public goods games and studies of cooperation and punishment) to illustrate how evolved human tendencies for cooperation, fairness, and punishment can be harnessed or undermined in organizational contexts. However, it does not provide new empirical findings or direct quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment or cooperation in public goods games or firm settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how evolutionary mechanisms (including punishment and cooperation) might affect efficiency and cooperation in group settings such as firms, referencing public goods game literature. However, it does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative estimates that can be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its value for prediction is mainly in highlighting the importance of both individual and group-level incentives, and the potential for punishment and reputational mechanisms to support cooperation, but it does not specify how game design dimensions quantitatively moderate these effects.

  findings: The paper argues that human cooperation on a large scale is an evolutionary anomaly, explained by a combination of multi-level selection and gene-culture co-evolution. It posits that mechanisms such as punishment, reward, and norm enforcement are universal features that help sustain cooperation, but the specific forms these take are historically and culturally contingent. The paper references empirical findings from public goods experiments and cross-cultural studies, noting that punishment (both altruistic and antisocial) is universal but varies in its effects on cooperation and group welfare. It also highlights that antisocial punishment can undermine group efficiency. However, the paper itself does not report new experimental results or quantify efficiency effects.

It suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not universal, but depends on the balance between altruistic and antisocial punishment, which is in turn influenced by local norms and institutional strength. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that game design dimensions (such as the presence and type of punishment, group size, and information structure) interact with cultural context to determine efficiency outcomes, but does not provide quantitative or directly predictive evidence.

- source: Artavia-Mora, L., Bedi, A. S., & Rieger, M. (2017). Intuitive help and punishment in the field. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that time delay (deliberation) reduces the likelihood of helping strangers and norm violators in the field, with helping rates dropping by 18-27 percentage points depending on the scenario. However, time delay does not significantly affect the rate of direct punishment of norm violators. The study also finds that time delay increases self-reported fear of retaliation for punishing. The design is a one-shot, non-strategic, individual-level field experiment, not a public goods game or its direct variant, and does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar strategic environments. Its design lacks group interaction, repeated rounds, contribution choices, or payoff structures analogous to PGGs. The main outcomes are binary helping or punishment actions in response to norm violations, not group payoffs or efficiency. While the findings may be contextually informative about the psychological underpinnings of prosocial and punitive behavior, they do not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games. The study is best used as contextual or mechanistic background, not as direct input for efficiency prediction models.

- source: Kaltwasser, L., Hildebrandt, A., Wilhelm, O., & Sommer, W. (2016). Behavioral and neuronal determinants of negative reciprocity in the ultimatum game. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both prosociality (as measured by social value orientation) and assertiveness predict the likelihood of rejecting unfair offers in the ultimatum game, supporting both the strong reciprocity and status defense models of negative reciprocity. Neural data (feedback negativity) show that individuals with higher negative reciprocity have stronger neural responses to proposer fairness. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not manipulate punishment as a treatment variable. The findings are limited to the behavioral and neural correlates of negative reciprocity in the UG, not to efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is an ultimatum game, not a public goods game, and the main outcome is the rejection rate of unfair offers (a form of costly punishment), not group payoff or efficiency. While the study is relevant for understanding individual motivations for costly punishment (negative reciprocity), it does not inform how punishment affects group efficiency or payoff under different game design dimensions. Thus, it should not be weighted strongly for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency outcomes.

  findings: The main empirical finding is that donors to an online fundraising platform are more likely to conceal their identity when making donations that are either much lower or much higher than the norm for a given appeal. The paper interprets this as evidence that individuals may hide norm-violating cooperative behavior (especially high donations) to avoid social costs such as punishment or ostracism, as has been observed in public goods game experiments. However, the study does not directly measure punishment, sanctions, or efficiency, and the context is real-world charitable giving rather than a controlled public goods game.

However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that social costs for norm-violating contributions (including over-contribution) may moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency outcomes. Its main value is in highlighting the potential for negative social responses to both under- and over-contribution.

- source: Szabó, G., Szolnoki, A., & Czakó, L. (2013). Coexistence of fraternity and egoism for spatial social dilemmas. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that allowing for fraternal (other-regarding) player types in spatial social dilemmas leads to the coexistence of egoist and fraternal strategies, often in spatially ordered patterns. The presence of fraternal players reduces the parameter region where only defectors survive, thus supporting higher levels of cooperation and higher average payoffs. The model demonstrates that spatial structure and personality diversity can promote cooperation and mitigate the tragedy of the commons, even without explicit punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms introducing other-regarding preferences (such as fraternity) can increase average group payoff and reduce the prevalence of inefficient, all-defect outcomes in spatial social dilemmas. However, the model is not a public goods game and does not include punishment or reward treatments. The findings suggest that, in environments where player types or preferences can vary, the introduction of pro-social types can have a positive effect on efficiency, analogous to the effect of some institutional interventions. For direct prediction of punishment effects in PGGs, this paper is only weakly informative, but it supports the general principle that mechanisms reducing pure self-interest can improve efficiency.

This effect is robust to controls for neuroticism and stress. Promotion focus does not show a similar effect. The behavioral outcomes are individual-level donations or transfers, not group-level efficiency or payoff. The study does not use a public goods game or any punishment/sanction mechanism.

It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or group payoff/efficiency. Its findings are only weakly relevant: they suggest that reputational cues can increase prosocial behavior for prevention-focused individuals, but this is shown in donation and dictator game contexts, not in PGGs or with punishment. The paper may inform understanding of individual differences in response to reputation cues, but does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Panchanathan, K., Frankenhuis, W. E., & Silk, J. B. (2013). The bystander effect in an N-person dictator game. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds a strong bystander effect in an N-person dictator game: when there is only one dictator, recipients receive significantly more than when there are two or three dictators. The average transfer as a fraction of the 'equal share' drops from 57% (one dictator) to 36% (two or three dictators). Recipient payoffs are about 50% higher in the one-dictator condition than in the two- or three-dictator conditions. This effect persists even when uncertainty about others' actions is removed (strategy method) and when dictators can communicate and coordinate (bargaining method). The results suggest that most participants transform the situation into a prisoner's dilemma, leading to low transfers in groups. The paper does not study punishment or sanctions, but discusses the possibility that introducing punishment could alleviate the bystander effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing the number of potential contributors in a many-to-one helping game (adjacent to PGG) reduces efficiency/welfare, even when communication or strategy methods are used. The absence of punishment or sanctions means the paper does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does establish a robust baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency declines with group size. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, in similar games without punishment, efficiency will be lower as group size increases. It also suggests that communication alone may not be sufficient to restore efficiency. However, since punishment is not present, the paper cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment, only to set a control baseline.

- source: McClung, J. S., Placì, S., Bangerter, A., Clément, F., & Bshary, R. (2017). The language of cooperation: shared intentionality drives variation in helping as a function of group membership. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: This paper introduces a joint foraging game (the 'egg hunt') in which pairs of participants can help each other at a cost to themselves, with helping not explicitly instructed. The design manipulates group membership (in-group vs out-group) and the ability to talk (talking vs no talking). Both in-group membership and the ability to talk significantly increased costly helping and overall group success (total screws collected, which determined monetary payoffs). Shared intentionality in conversation was identified as the key psychological mechanism mediating the effect of group membership on helping. Costly helping was costly to the helper but increased total group payoff. There were no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. The game is adjacent to public goods or prisoner's dilemma games, but differs in that helping is not explicitly structured and there is no punishment or reward system.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that enabling communication (chat) and making group membership salient can increase efficiency (total group payoff) in joint action games where helping is possible but not enforced. The effect is mediated by shared intentionality in communication. While the game is not a standard public goods game and does not include punishment, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency in games where communication and group identity are manipulated. The absence of punishment means the paper does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does show that communication alone can have a strong positive effect on efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the importance of chat/communication and group identity, but does not provide evidence about punishment, punishment cost, or related dimensions.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2016). Imperfect information facilitates the evolution of reciprocity. *Mathematical Biosciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of imperfect information on the evolution of reciprocity (and thus on group efficiency/payoff) depends on the structure of payoffs. When payoffs are linear, imperfect information always makes reciprocity (cooperation) less likely to evolve, reducing group efficiency. However, when payoffs are nonlinear (synergetic effects), imperfect information can sometimes make reciprocity more likely to evolve, increasing group efficiency. The analysis is theoretical, based on evolutionary stability conditions for tit-for-tat (TFT) versus always-defect (ALLD) strategies in the iterated prisoner's dilemma with mistakes and imperfect information. The results do not generalize directly to public goods games or to settings with punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how information structure and payoff nonlinearity can affect the evolution of cooperation and group efficiency in repeated dyadic games. However, it does not address public goods games, punishment, or sanctions, and thus is only indirectly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The main lesson is that the effect of information and error structure on efficiency can be non-monotonic and depend on payoff nonlinearity, but this does not directly inform the effect of punishment in multi-player public goods games.

- source: Chiang, Y. S. (2015). Good Samaritans in Networks: An Experiment on How Networks Influence Egalitarian Sharing and the Evolution of Inequality. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that voluntary sharing in networks can reduce inequality, but the extent depends on network topology. When networks connect the very rich and very poor (Lattice_Hetero, SF_Negative), local inequality is salient and motivates more redistribution, leading to significant reductions in the Gini coefficient. In other network structures (Full, Lattice_Homo, SF_Positive), inequality is not significantly reduced. The study does not involve public goods provision, punishment, or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency as the ratio of group payoff to the fully cooperative benchmark. The findings are most relevant to how network structure moderates voluntary redistribution and the evolution of inequality.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency in the sense required for the downstream prediction task. The findings are about voluntary redistribution and inequality in networked settings, which is adjacent but not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The paper may provide some contextual insight into how network structure affects redistribution, but it does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Molleman, L., Pen, I., & Weissing, F. J. (2013). Effects of Conformism on the Cultural Evolution of Social Behaviour. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of conformism on the evolution of cooperation depends on the game structure. In social dilemmas, conformism can make cooperation a stable equilibrium if it is already common, but in group-structured populations, conformism hinders the spread of cooperation because it prevents rare cooperative strategies from invading. In evasion games, conformism erodes polymorphic equilibria and leads to fixation of a single strategy. The main outcomes are fixation probabilities and average payoffs at equilibrium, which are related to efficiency. The paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or reward mechanisms, and does not directly model public goods games, but the results are relevant for understanding how social learning biases affect group-level cooperation and welfare.

  findings: The paper finds that introducing delay-based rewards (where the reward for cooperation is discounted by the time delay before it is received) can significantly increase the level of cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, especially on square lattice networks. The effect is strongest when the difference in reward between different delays is intermediate (i.e., not too small or too large). The cooperation level is maximized at a specific discounting factor (K ≈ 1.0). The effect is weaker in random and small-world networks. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, and does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms.

  findings: The paper finds that introducing mutation into the evolutionary dynamics of the repeated Prisoner's Dilemma (with ALLC, ALLD, and costly TFT) can robustly generate stable limit cycles in the population, resulting in oscillations between cooperation and defection. These cycles occur for a wide range of mutation structures and parameter values, and can be sparked by arbitrarily small mutation rates and complexity costs. The presence of these cycles means that cooperation can persist at substantial levels, even though defection would otherwise dominate. However, the analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include punishment, reward, or explicit efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of mutation and strategy diversity for sustaining cooperation, which may be contextually informative for broader models of cooperation.

  findings: The main finding is that in a spatial Ultimatum Game, when proposers allocate more chips to less successful opponents (negative correlation), the population evolves toward higher average offers and acceptance thresholds, interpreted as greater fairness. When more chips are allocated to more successful opponents (positive correlation), fairness is reduced. The effect is robust across different network topologies and is amplified by broader group interaction. The study does not address efficiency, group payoff, or punishment, and is not a public goods game, but provides adjacent evidence about how resource allocation rules affect fairness-related behavioral outcomes.

  findings: The study finds that children's cooperation rates are positively associated with parental education and parents' perception of their child's superiority. No evidence is provided on efficiency or group payoff, and no punishment or sanctioning is present in the design.

  findings: The study finds that participants are more likely to help ingroup than outgroup members, and more likely to help when decisions are public (i.e., reputational concerns are present). However, ingroup favouritism is observed even in private (anonymous) conditions, suggesting a role for social preferences beyond reputation management. The effect of public/private decision setting is stronger in loss contexts than gain contexts, but the pattern of ingroup favouritism is similar in both. The cost/benefit (net expected value) of helping influences overall helping rates, but does not moderate the effect of group membership or decision setting. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.

- source: Lu, P., & Wang, F. (2015). Heterogeneity of inferring reputation probability in cooperative behaviors for the spatial prisoners' dilemma game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak

  findings: The study finds that the mean probability with which agents can correctly identify and imitate the most reputable neighbor ('inferring reputation probability') has a nonlinear effect on cooperation: increasing the mean from low values (<0.5) strongly increases cooperation, but further increases stabilize or even reduce cooperation. Heterogeneity in this probability (standard deviation) has effects that depend on the mean: it can reduce cooperation when the mean is low, but promote cooperation when the mean is high. All results are in terms of cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are modeled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. - source: Capraro, V., & Marcelletti, A. (2014). Do good actions inspire good actions in others?. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that good actions (altruism or benevolence) do not inspire further good actions in anonymous, one-shot economic games. Specifically, being the recipient of a good action (either a split in a Dictator Game or a benevolent act in a Benevolence Game) does not significantly increase the probability of cooperating in a subsequent Prisoner's Dilemma or splitting in a subsequent Dictator Game. The results are robust to exclusion of participants who did not believe the other player was real or failed to provide a valid reason for their choice. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Its findings on the lack of behavioral contagion of good actions in one-shot, anonymous settings may be contextually relevant for understanding baseline cooperation or altruism, but not for the prediction of efficiency under punishment treatments.

- source: Andrighetto, G., Grieco, D., & Tummolini, L. (2015). Perceived legitimacy of normative expectations motivates compliance with social norms when nobody is watching. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that communication (pre-play messages) makes social norms salient and increases both trust (A's willingness to enter the game) and trustworthiness (B's willingness to ROLL, i.e., act cooperatively). The presence of a costly EXIT option allows Bs to avoid public norm violation by deceiving As, helping to distinguish between motivations for compliance. The key result is that, when no monitoring or punishment is possible, compliance is driven by the perceived legitimacy of normative expectations rather than by guilt aversion or fear of losing esteem. The study does not report or analyze efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. Its main relevance is in showing that, in the absence of punishment, communication and the salience of social norms can increase cooperative behavior (trust/trustworthiness) in a trust game. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that, even without punishment, norm compliance can be achieved through perceived legitimacy of normative expectations, but it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Pan, X. S., & Houser, D. (2013). Cooperation during cultural group formation promotes trust towards members of out-groups. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the degree of cooperation required during group formation (cooperative vs. independent production tasks) causally affects trust and reciprocity in subsequent trust games. Specifically, participants from cooperative production groups (CP) trust and reciprocate with out-group members at similar rates as with in-group members, showing little parochial altruism. In contrast, participants from independent production groups (IP) display significant in-group favouritism and out-group discrimination. These effects persist even after negative experiences (betrayal) in the trust game. The results are explained by differences in beliefs about out-group trustworthiness, shaped by the group formation process. No efficiency, payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that the cooperative context of group formation can influence trust and reciprocity, but not in a payoff or efficiency framework. It should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The study finds that cooperation in both regions is grounded in small, personal trust groups rather than being homogeneously distributed within cultural groups. Northern Ghana, with greater environmental scarcity, exhibits larger and more inclusive trust and cooperation networks, more openness to newcomers, and less emphasis on punishment for betrayal of trust compared to Oaxaca. In Oaxaca, trust and cooperation are more restricted to close relatives, and betrayal of trust is more likely to be punished or not forgiven. The paper argues that environmental scarcity fosters broader, more cohesive trust networks. However, the study does not use experimental games, does not manipulate punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes.

It does not use game-theoretic or experimental methods, nor does it report group payoff, efficiency, or related outcomes. The discussion of punishment is limited to informal, social responses to betrayal of trust, not to formal sanctioning mechanisms. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, though it may provide some anthropological context about the role of trust and informal punishment in real-world cooperation.

  findings: The paper finds that minimal eye-like patterns (eyespots) on butterflies increase their perceived attractiveness and elicit more positive attitudes toward their conservation. In some cases, eyespots also increase the likelihood of actual conservation behavior (e.g., donations), though this effect is mixed. The effect of eyespots on conservation attitudes is mediated by increased aesthetic preference. The study does not address economic games, punishment, or group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. - source: Cai, W., Huang, X. Q., Wu, S., & Kou, Y. (2015). Dishonest behavior is not affected by an image of watching eyes. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  findings: The main finding is that implicit reputation cues (images of watching eyes) do not reduce dishonest behavior in laboratory settings, even when dishonesty leads to higher monetary payoffs or reputational benefits. This null effect was consistent across different tasks and motivational framings. The study also finds that higher moral identity predicts less dishonesty, but the eyes image does not affect this. The pilot experiment replicates the known effect of eyes images on generosity in a dictator game, but the main experiments focus on dishonesty, not cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, nor does it use a PGG or close variant. The findings about implicit reputation cues and dishonesty may be of tangential interest for understanding the limits of non-punitive interventions, but do not inform the core prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Hackman, J., Munira, S., Jasmin, K., & Hruschka, D. (2017). Revisiting Psychological Mechanisms in the Anthropology of Altruism. *Human Nature-an Interdisciplinary Biosocial Perspective*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that in four rural Bangladeshi communities, perceived social closeness is a strong and independent predictor of helping behavior between households, even after controlling for genetic relatedness, affinal ties, and geographic proximity. The odds of helping are much higher for those rated as very close, and closeness explains a substantial portion of the association between helping and other variables. Friendship nominations also predict helping, but closeness remains a robust predictor even when both are included. The study does not use any experimental or game-theoretic design, and does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or reward.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not use a PGG or any economic game, does not manipulate or analyze punishment, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings about social closeness and helping in naturalistic social networks may be of theoretical interest for understanding cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in PGG-like games.
